Solomonoff Function Induction:
Definitions, Versions and Basic Theorems
Kaarel Hänni∗
October 2026

Abstract
Solomonoff function induction (SFI) is Bayesian prediction of labels from input–output
examples under a universal prior over random functions: a random program, reading random
bits shared by all inputs, labels every input. A universal function machine exists; its law
dominates the law of every function machine and does not depend, up to a constant factor,
on the universal machine or on computable encodings. The shortest consistent program, the
program mixture, the mixture of product laws, SFI and the universal weak semimeasure agree
up to K(dom D) bits, but no two of the first three agree up to a constant: there is no coding
theorem for function programs. On every consistent data sequence, SFI’s cumulative log loss
exceeds that of every computable random labeller by at most the labeller’s complexity plus a
constant; with i.i.d. inputs from any distribution and computable labels, the averaged predictor
has risk O(K/n). SFI cannot model fresh label noise on repeated inputs; a per-query mixture
can. SFI cannot use the order of the data: on a random cyclic permutation revealed along
its cycle it pays about (d − log2 e)2d bits in the semimeasure convention, while Solomonoff
induction on the interleaved sequence pays O(1) in expectation. Hypotheses that see the list
of previous inputs give the universal chronological semimeasure. A dominance theorem for
computable reveal orders holds in its intended reading, though an earlier proof sketch fails.
Further sections treat Sam Eisenstat’s coin example, out-of-distribution behaviour, computable
versions and neural-network Bayes. A companion paper treats steeper simplicity priors.

How the claims were checked

1

Introduction

In supervised learning a learner sees input–output pairs (x1 , y1 ), (x2 , y2 ), . . . and predicts the
output at a new input. For predicting sequences, Solomonoff induction (SI) is the standard ideal
reference: a Bayes mixture over all computable sources whose cumulative log loss exceeds that
of each computable source by at most a constant that depends on the source (Li and Vitányi,
2008). Hänni (2026b) proposes the analogous object for supervised learning, Solomonoff function
induction (SFI): “you think of the input-output pairs as being generated by picking a random
program from this prior and generating using it, and so you update on seen pairs as you should”.
This paper defines SFI carefully, proves its basic properties, checks the claims about it made in
those notes, and works out examples.
Why SFI. Supervised learning is central to machine learning, and SFI is a candidate for its ideal
form. It is the Bayes predictor for the prior “a random lower-semicomputable labeller”. On every
sequence of pairs that some function could produce, its cumulative log loss exceeds that of every
computable random labeller by at most that labeller’s complexity plus a constant (Theorem 4.1).
Since SFI never predicts the inputs, this holds for every input distribution, including adversarial
and non-computable ones; with i.i.d. inputs it gives the Occam rate O(K/n) for the averaged
predictor, without a union bound (Theorem 4.5). The analogy has limits, which we make precise:
the guarantees are for log loss and for averaged predictors; a random function cannot produce
fresh label noise on a repeated input; SFI is not computable; and SFI cannot use the order in
which the data arrive (Remark 4.16).
Relation to Solomonoff induction. Up to constant factors, SI is SFI on the inputs
0, 1, . . . , n − 1 (Proposition 2.23). In general, though, SFI sees the data as a set: each hypothesis
answers each query without seeing the other inputs, and SFI’s cumulative loss in the semimeasure
convention does not depend on the order of the data (Proposition 2.16). SI applied to the
interleaved sequence x1 y1 x2 y2 · · · sees the order and can do much better (Sections 5 and 6).
Giving SFI’s hypotheses the list of previous inputs closes this gap: the result, SFIhist , is the
universal chronological semimeasure (Corollary 5.18). With inputs as actions and labels as
percepts this is the kind of object that underlies Hutter’s AIXI (Hutter, 2000, 2005); we have not
checked the identification against Hutter’s texts.
Relation to neural-network Bayes. Bayesian learning with a neural network is functionspace Bayes with the prior that the parameter prior induces on functions (Proposition 9.1).
Vaintrob and Hänni (2026b) argue that under a strong weight prior this prior roughly tracks
a circuit complexity of the function, so that NN Bayes behaves like a circuit version of SFI.
Sections 8 and 9 prove what can be proved in general about circuit SFI and about this link, and
quote the slides’ main claims as theirs.

1.1

Contributions

• Definitions and universality (Section 2). Function machines read one random tape shared
by all inputs. A universal function machine exists, and its law MF (SFI) dominates every
machine law, including lower-semicomputable product laws, partial computable functions and
laws of random functions with computable cylinder probabilities (Theorem 2.9 and Proposition 2.10). The one-point inequality alone defines a strictly larger class, whose universal
3

element exceeds MF by a number of bits linear in the size of some data sets (Theorem 2.13).
We prove invariance under the universal machine and under computable encodings (Theorem 2.20), coding-theorem bounds (Theorem 2.21) and non-computability (Proposition 2.24),
and we separate the semimeasure convention for losses from the normalised one (Proposition 2.17 and Remark 2.18).
• Kolmogorov and semimeasure versions (Section 3). The shortest consistent program KF , the
program mixture W , the product mixture Mprod , SFI and the weak universal semimeasure
agree up to K(dom D) + O(1) bits (Theorem 3.4), but no two of KF , W , MF agree up to
a constant. There is no coding theorem for function programs: on some two-point data sets
KF ≥ log d − 2 while − log W is O(log log d) (Theorem 3.9 and Corollary 3.10). W falls short
of MF only by the padded complexity of the needed tape length (Proposition 3.6). Table 2
lists every comparison.
• Learning theorems (Section 4). Pathwise dominance (Theorem 4.1), merging (Theorem 4.3), a
mistake bound (Corollary 4.4) and online-to-batch bounds (Theorem 4.5). Under fresh label
noise with repeated inputs, random-function SFI has infinite expected loss (Proposition 4.6);
time stamps, frozen noise or a per-query mixture repair this (Theorem 4.5 and Proposition 4.7).
The expected risk of a Bayes predictor need not decrease with more data (Proposition 4.8);
per-step in-distribution error bounds are in Corollary 4.14.
• History-aware prediction (Section 5). On Hänni’s chain and skipped-string examples SFI’s loss
grows quadratically in the number of blocks when their lengths are 1, 2, 3, . . . , in both conventions up to lower-order terms, while a constant-size set-based predictor has loss 0, and every
order-independent predictor pays the same lower bounds (Theorems 5.3 and 5.4 and Propositions 5.5 and 5.6). Hänni’s dominance theorem for computable reveal laws is true in the
reading his text supports, under finiteness hypotheses; its proof sketch fails, and a stronger
reading is false (Proposition 5.8, Corollary 5.14, and Theorem 5.12). SFIhist is the universal
chronological semimeasure (Theorem 5.17 and Corollary 5.18); its exact relation to SI on the
interleaved sequence is in Theorem 5.20 and Proposition 5.21.
• Worked examples (Section 6). For a random cyclic permutation of {0, 1}d revealed along its
cycle, SFI pays log((N − 1)!) ≈ (d − log e)2d bits with high probability in the semimeasure
convention, and in the normalised convention at least δd − O(log d) bits per step in expectation
for the first 2(1−δ)d steps, while SI pays O(1) in expectation and SFIhist pays O(1) on every
instance, uniformly in d (Theorem 6.3 and Propositions 6.6, 6.7 and 6.9). In Sam Eisenstat’s
coin example SFI pays H(1/3)
n ± O(log n) bits in the semimeasure convention, which is optimal
2
among order-independent predictors up to O(log n), while SI pays at most 2.4 bits plus a
constant in expectation (Theorems 6.15 and 6.19 and Proposition 6.16). Positive examples are
in Section 6.4.
• Out-of-distribution behaviour (Section 7). An unseen simple property gives every simple behaviour on it a posterior probability bounded below, with no logarithmic term and independently of the amount of data (Proposition 7.1), and the bound depends on the universal
machine (Proposition 7.4). Over any sequence of distinct inputs, the total posterior error on a
computable target f is at most (ln 2)(K(f ) + c) (Theorem 7.7). The naive converse fails, and
a converse given the labelled sample holds (Propositions 7.10 and 7.12).
• Computable versions and networks (Sections 8 and 9). Time-bounded mixtures have constant regret against all predictors of a given running time (Theorem 8.2, Proposition 8.4,
4

and Corollary 8.5). For circuit Bayes, mistakes and circuit size are polynomially related on
every ordering of the data (Theorem 8.10), while simplest-consistent (Occam) learning on the
same prior makes 2Ω(ℓ/ log ℓ) mistakes on some ordering for a target of description length ℓ
(Theorem 8.14). For networks we prove the general facts about the induced prior and a separation between Gaussian-process limits and SFI on parities (Propositions 9.2, 9.4 and 9.5,
Corollary 9.8, and Remark 9.9).
• Steeper simplicity priors (Section 10) summarises the companion paper (Hänni, 2026a): when
a steeper penalty on program length keeps a learner from reproducing a teacher’s systematic
errors, and when it prefers a simple sampler to a model with filled-in random bits.
Corrections to earlier claims. Several claims of Hänni (2026b) are corrected here; the text
says so where each appears. The proof sketch of the dominance theorem for reveal laws fails,
although the theorem holds in its intended reading (Proposition 5.8 and Corollary 5.14). The twosided bounds on out-of-distribution generalisation in the PDF draft are false as literally stated;
the intended statement holds (Proposition 7.6 and Theorem 7.5). The converse asked about in
the notes, “no simple separating property implies good generalisation”, fails unless the bound
may depend on K(f ) (Propositions 7.10 and 7.12). The Kolmogorov and semimeasure versions,
which the draft hoped to relate by a coding theorem, are not equivalent (Remark 3.14). The
“physicist’s proof” of few online mistakes is a proof for weighted majority, not for Occam learning
(Remark 8.13 and Theorem 8.14). The e-mail argument for Eisenstat’s example is correct in the
semimeasure convention after two repairs (Remark 6.17). Table 1 lists the ten working hypotheses
with which this study began and what became of each.
Credit. The notion of SFI, many of the claims examined here and most of the examples come
from Hänni (2026b) and Hänni (2026c): the dominance template for samplers that see only the
input, the remark on input encodings, the chain and skipped-string examples, the dominance
theorem for reveal laws, the conjecture on hypotheses that see the previous inputs, the cyclicpermutation example, the out-of-distribution proposition and examples, and the time-bounded
and circuit versions. The coin example of Section 6.3 is due to Sam Eisenstat, as reported in Hänni
(2026c). The material on neural-network Bayes, including the conjunction ladder of Theorem 8.14,
follows the slides of Vaintrob and Hänni (2026b). Hutter’s universal chronological semimeasure
and his treatment of supervised learning as a problem class for AIXI (Hutter, 2000, 2005, 2007)
are prior work in substance for Section 5; we have checked them only at the level of abstracts and
tables of contents (Remark 4.17 and Section 11).
What is not achieved. Several natural questions stay open: whether MF dominates every
lower-semicomputable law of a random partial function (Section 2.3); the exact size of the gap
between W and MF (Section 3.4); several lower bounds in the normalised convention (Sections 2.4
and 6.3); whether the K(n) + K(A) term in the dominance theorem for reveal laws can be
removed (Remark D.6); an O(K/n) bound for the last iterate rather than the average (Remark 4.9
and Conjecture 4.12); efficient time-bounded learning against samplers (Section 8.1); and a twosided quantitative bridge between NN Bayes and circuit SFI, of which one half is proved for
uniform circuit priors (Proposition 9.5 and Section 9). The main theorems of the slides are not
checked here. The constants in our bounds depend on the universal machine and can be large,
and SFI itself is not computable, so the results describe an ideal, not an algorithm. Much of the
literature was seen only through abstracts and secondary sources (Section 11).

6

Reader’s guide. Section 2 is the base for everything else. Section 3 answers the question of
the Kolmogorov and semimeasure versions and can be skipped on a first reading. Section 4 gives
the learning theorems. Sections 5 and 6 show where SFI and SI differ and how history-aware
hypotheses remove the difference. Section 7 treats out-of-distribution behaviour, Sections 8 and 9
computable versions and networks, Section 10 the companion paper, and Sections 11 and 12
related work and open problems. Each main section states its results with short proof ideas; full
proofs are in Appendices A to H. Two conventions for losses recur: the semimeasure convention
scores SFI’s raw predictive sub-probabilities, and the normalised convention rescales them to
sum to one at each step. Upper bounds in the first hold in both; every lower bound says which
convention it is for (Remark 2.18). Logarithms are to base 2, losses are in bits, and c denotes a
constant that depends only on the fixed universal machines and encodings.

2

Function semimeasures, function machines and SFI

Hänni’s definition of Solomonoff function induction (SFI) reads: “you have a bunch of inputoutput pairs. you have the usual prior on all programs. solomonoff function induction := you
think of the input-output pairs as being generated by picking a random program from this prior
and generating using it, and so you update on seen pairs as you should” (Hänni, 2026b). A
random program may be deterministic or read random bits, private to each input or shared by all
inputs. We take the most general reading that still yields a random function: one random tape
read by all inputs (Definition 2.7); Section 3 compares the other readings. The notion and several
of the claims checked below are Hänni’s. Proofs are in Appendix A.

2.1

Data and conventions

Definition 2.1 (Data). foundations §1 Inputs X, labels Y ; by default X = Y = {0, 1}∗ , but any
infinite decidable X and countable Y , |Y | ≥ 2, will do unless stated. ⊥ means “undefined”,
Y⊥ := Y ∪ {⊥}. A data set is a finite partial function D : X ⇀ Y ; D + (x, y) := D ∪ {(x, y)}
for x ∈
/ dom D; D|T is a restriction. A partial function f extends D, f ⊇ D, if f (x) = y
for all (x, y) ∈ D. A sequence (xt , yt )t≤n is consistent if Dn := {(xt , yt ) : t ≤ n} is a partial
function; ht := (x1 , y1 , . . . , xt , yt ) is the history. For ν from data sets to [0, 1] the conditional is
ν(y | D, x) := ν(D + (x, y))/ν(D) for x ∈
/ dom D (0 if ν(D) = 0), and ν(y | D, x) := 1[y = D(x)]
for x ∈ dom D (the repeated-input convention).
Complexity and constants. V is a fixed universal prefix machine, K prefix complexity, φe
P
the e-th partial computable function X ⇀ Y , m(i) := V (p)=i 2−|p| ≥ 2−K(i) , and K(ν) :=
min{K(i) : νi = ν} in an effective enumeration (νi ); “lsc” means lower semicomputable. We
use, without re-checking the sources (Levin, 1974; Gács, 1974; Chaitin, 1975), (Li and Vitányi,
2008, §4.3), that m dominates every lsc discrete semimeasure up to a constant factor, and the
P
conditional coding theorem: if P (a | z) is lsc uniformly in (a, z) with a P (a | z) ≤ 1, then
K(a | z) ≤ − log P (a | z) + K(P ) + c; both relativise. log = log2 ; losses are in bits; c and O(1)
depend only on the fixed machines and codings. U is the fair-coin measure on tapes ω ∈ {0, 1}∞ ,
and ω1:L the first L tape bits.
Two loss conventions. The predictors below issue sub-probabilities; the missing mass is the
probability of “undefined”. The semimeasure convention scores the raw conditionals, the normalised convention divides each by its sum. Statements about losses name their convention; up7

per bounds in the semimeasure convention hold in both, lower bounds need not (Proposition 2.17
and Remark 2.18).

2.2

Three notions of function semimeasure

Definition 2.2 (Weak function semimeasure). foundations Def 2.1 A map ν from data sets to [0, 1]
P
with ν(∅) ≤ 1 and ν(D) ≥ y∈Y ν(D + (x, y)) for every D and x ∈
/ dom D.
Then ν is monotone, and D:dom D=S ν(D) ≤ ν(∅) for finite S (sum out one input at a
time). Call a predictor q (sub-probabilities q(· | D, x), with the repeated-input convention) orderindependent if, for every D, the product νq (D) of its conditionals along a reveal order of D is the
same for all orders.
P

Proposition 2.3 (Predictor reading). [proved] foundations Prop 2.1a (= context Prop 5.1(a)) (a) If ν is
weak with ν(∅) = 1, its conditionals form an order-independent predictor qν with νqν = ν. (b) If
q is order-independent, νq is weak with νq (∅) = 1, and q(y | D, x) = νq (D + (x, y))/νq (D) when
νq (D) > 0. So weak semimeasures with ν(∅) = 1 correspond one-to-one to order-independent
predictors, identified where ν(D) > 0.
Definition 2.4 (Random partial function).
X (product σ-algebra) with
foundations Def 2.2 A random partial function is a random element F of Y⊥
law P ; its containment functional is νP (D) := P (F ⊇ D).
Every containment functional is weak, since the events {F ⊇ D + (x, y)}, y ∈ Y , are disjoint
subsets of {F ⊇ D}. The converse fails (Example 2.6). If every conditional of q sums to 1, νq is the
law of a random total function, and MF dominates every such lsc law (Proposition A.1 and CorolP
lary A.3, Appendix A.1). For finite T ⊆ X \ dom D put ∇T ν(D) := E: dom E⊆T (−1)|E| ν(D ∪ E).
Proposition 2.5 (Möbius characterisation). [proved] foundations Prop 2.3 (= context Prop 5.1(c)); checks c6,
r4 A weak ν with ν(∅) = 1 is the containment functional of a random partial function if and
only if ∇T ν(D) ≥ 0 for all D and all finite T ⊆ X \ dom D. The law is then unique, and
∇T ν(D) = P (F ⊇ D, F |T ≡ ⊥).
The weak inequality is the case |T | = 1. The proposition is the function-space analogue of
the correspondence between completely monotone set functions and random sets (Choquet, 1954;
Matheron, 1975; Shafer, 1976) [known; unverified]; we have not checked the analogy against these
sources.
Example 2.6 (Weak but not a random function). [proved; computed] foundations Ex 2.4; checks c1, c6,
c9(b), r1, r4 (a) Inputs a, b: ν(∅) = ν({(a, 0)}) = ν({(b, 0)}) = 1, all other values 0. It is weak, but
∇{a,b} ν(∅) = −1: “F (a) = 0 surely and F (b) = 0 surely, but never both”. (b) Inputs a, b, c, labels
{0, 1}: ν△ (∅) = 1, and ν△ = 21 on the six one-point data sets and on E1 = {(a, 0), (b, 1)}, E2 =
{(b, 0), (c, 1)}, E3 = {(c, 0), (a, 1)}, and 0 elsewhere. ν△ is weak (c1), and E1 , E2 , E3 are pairwise
inconsistent with total mass 23 ; so no law P of a random partial function has P (F ⊇ ·) ≥ c ν△
with c > 23 , and the uniform law on (F (a), F (b), F (c)) ∈ {(0, 1, 0), (1, 0, 1), (1, 0, 0)} attains c = 32 .

2.3

Function machines and universality

Definition 2.7 (Function machine).

foundations Def 2.5 A function machine is a c.e. set T ⊆ X × {0, 1}∗ × Y of triples that is consistent:
if (x, w, y), (x, w′ , y ′ ) ∈ T and w, w′ are comparable, then y = y ′ . For a tape ω put fωT (x) := y

8

if some prefix w of ω has (x, w, y) ∈ T , and fωT (x) := ⊥ otherwise. The machine law of T is
νT (D) := U{ω : fωT ⊇ D}.
Equivalently, T is a Turing machine with a one-way random tape which on query x reads
finitely many tape bits and halts with a label or diverges; all queries read the same tape.
Proposition 2.8 (Machine laws). [proved]
foundations Prop 2.6 and the enumeration remark Every machine law is the containment functional of a
random partial function, with νT (∅) = 1, and is lsc uniformly in D and an index of T . Function
machines have an effective enumeration (Ti ), to which K(ν) for machine laws refers.

Q

In (a) each input samples from a private stream of tape bits, as Hänni proposed: “use different
bits of randomness from the universal semimeasure’s infinite read tape for sampling on different
inputs; one can do this using dovetailing” (Hänni, 2026b). With Theorem 2.9 this gives his
dominance theorem for such samplers (Theorem 4.1).
Remark 2.11 (Per input, not per query). foundations remark after Prop 3.3 In (a) a repeated query returns
the same label; a sampler run afresh at each query would not. This choice fits Hänni’s setting
of “input-output pairs from a function” and makes νP a random function; the per-query reading
gives a different object, Mpq (Definition 3.1 and Proposition 4.7).
Theorem 2.12 (Universal weak semimeasure). [proved; trimming computed] foundations Thm 3.4; check
r3 There is an lsc weak semimeasure M w with M w ≥ 2−K(ν) ν for every lsc weak semimeasure ν.
Theorem 2.13 (Weak versus machine universality). [proved; computed]
foundations Thm 3.5 and the remark after it; checks c1, c6, c9, r1 (i) MF ≤ 2O(1) M w . (ii) For every lsc weak
ν and every D, MF (D) ≥ 2−K(ν)−K(dom D)−O(1) ν(D); so M w (D) ≤ 2K(dom D)+O(1) MF (D). (iii)
For every k some Dk with | dom Dk | = 2k has log(M w (Dk )/MF (Dk )) ≥ k log 32 − K(k) − O(1).
(iv) This bound holds on average over the 3k explicit data sets E⃗ı of the proof. (v) The constant
⊗k
(2/3)k behind (iii) is exact for ν△

In (ii) the labels of dom D, read in a fixed order, form a semimeasure on strings that a monotone
machine realises exactly (Lemma A.5). In (iii) k blocks of ν△ give 3k pairwise inconsistent data
sets of M w -mass ≥ 2−k−K(k)−O(1) each, while MF gives them total mass ≤ 1; there K(dom Dk ) ≤
k log 3 + K(k) + O(1), so at least a fraction log 23 / log 3 ≈ 0.37 of the term K(dom D) is needed.
Remark 2.14 (Weak and machine universality differ). [refuted (weak definition); open (random-function
laws)] foundations §3, claim F10 A Levin-style equivalence “lsc function semimeasures, or equivalently

monotone function machines” (a natural conjecture, by analogy with sequences (Zvonkin and
Levin, 1970)) is refuted for the weak definition: M w and MF agree up to 2K(dom D)+O(1) , not up
to a constant. For random-function laws it is open (Section 2.3).
Open question. Is every lsc law of a random partial function dominated by MF up to a
constant factor? Yes for the classes of Proposition 2.10 and for computable filling-in processes
(Remark A.4); up to 2K(dom D)+O(1) always. It would suffice (proof sketch, Appendix A.6) that
every such law has lower approximations increasing on all up-sets of the extension order, not only
on the sets {F ⊇ D}; Strassen’s theorem (Strassen, 1965) then gives couplings that fill in values.

2.4

The definition of SFI and its conventions

Definition 2.15 (Solomonoff function induction).

the posterior is the law of fω given fω ⊇ D, with containment functional E 7→ MF (D∪E)/MF (D).

/ dom D is MF (y | D, x) = MF (D + (x, y))/MF (D); its defect

P

conditional is M̄F (y | D, x) := MF (D + (x, y))/Z(D, x). For a weak ν, Lν (D) := − log ν(D), and
SFI’s loss is LSFI (D) := LMF (D).
MF (D) > 0 for every D (the lookup table of D is a program).
Proposition 2.16 (Order independence). [proved; computed]
foundations Prop 5.2 (= context Fact A, history Prop 5.1); check c4 Semimeasure convention. For every weak
P
ν and consistent (xt , yt )t≤n , t≤n − log ν(yt | Dt−1 , xt ) = Lν (Dn ) + log ν(∅) ≤ Lν (Dn ), which
depends only on the set Dn , not on the order or on repetitions. For SFI it is LSFI (Dn ). On an
inconsistent sequence it is infinite.
(A repeated input contributes the factor 1 or 0; the other factors telescope.)
Proposition 2.17 (Normalisation). [proved; computed] foundations Prop 5.3; checks c3, r8, r13; context c3 Let
P
L̄SFI := t≤n − log M̄F (yt | Dt−1 , xt ) along a given consistent sequence. (a) L̄SFI ≤ LSFI (Dn ),
so upper bounds on LSFI hold in both conventions. (b) For laws of random partial functions the
normalised loss can depend on the order. (c) For random total functions it equals the semimeasure
loss.
For (b), let (F (a), F (b)) be uniform on {(0, 0), (0, ⊥), (1, 1), (⊥, ⊥)}: D = {(a, 0), (b, 0)} has
normalised probability 32 if a comes first and 12 if b does, semimeasure probability 14 in both orders
(c3). On a random six-point mixture the semimeasure loss is 10.919980315195 bits in all 720
orders, the normalised loss between 7.33 and 10.64 bits (context c3).
What does transfer is collected in Proposition A.7 (Appendix A.7): with Zt the posterior

transfer; and two general lower bounds, K ∅ (y1:n | x1:n ) − O(1) and the chronological Bayes loss,

Remark 2.18 (Which lower bounds are semimeasure-only). [proved (as a list); transfer open where
stated] foundations Rem 5.6, updated by history Prop 2.4, examples Prop 2.9, context §1.2 Lower bounds on LSFI (Dn ),
or for every order-free predictor, do not transfer automatically to L̄SFI . In this paper they hold in
the semimeasure convention only for: Theorem 2.21(a) beyond single points; Example 2.22; the
GF(2q ) example (Theorem 5.26); the separations of Propositions 5.19 and D.11; the cyclic permutation over the full cycle (Theorem 6.3); Eisenstat’s example (Theorems 6.13 and 6.15). Also normalised: the chain and the skipped strings, up to O(n log n) and O(ℓ log ℓ) bits (Proposition 5.6);
the cyclic permutation at steps t ≤ 2(1−δ)d , at δd − O(log d) bits per step (Proposition 6.6). In
both conventions: the out-of-distribution lower bounds of Section 7, which bound the posterior
mass that is defined and wrong.
Open question. Is the normalised loss of MF order-dependent, and which semimeasure-only
lower bounds hold for M̄F ? On two inputs the exact condition is the criterion (⋆) of Proposition A.6: the normaliser leaks order information exactly when whether one input is defined says
something about another input’s label. A failure of (⋆) for MF is expected but not proved.
Remark 2.19 (Recommendation).
foundations §5 “Recommendation” We call MF “SFI”: it is the object Hänni describes; it has a posterior
over functions (M w has none); it dominates every machine law with constant 2−K(ν) ; it is invariant
(Theorem 2.20); its semimeasure-convention loss is order-independent. The variants W , KF ,
Mprod , Mpq , M w (Section 3) agree with it up to K(dom D) + O(1) bits, differ by unbounded
amounts, and should be kept distinct.

2.5

Basic properties

Theorem 2.20 (Invariance). [proved]
′ are built from two universal prefix machines and two enumerations
foundations Thm 6.2 (a) If MF , MF
of function machines, | log MF (D) − log MF′ (D)| ≤ c for all D. (b) For computable injections
α : X → X ′ , β : Y → Y ′ , with Dαβ := {(α(x), β(y)) : (x, y) ∈ D} and MF′ SFI over X ′ , Y ′ :
| log MF (D) − log MF′ (Dαβ )| ≤ K(α, β) + O(1) for all D. So cumulative losses on original and
re-encoded data differ by at most K(α, β) + O(1) bits, uniformly over data sets and reveal orders.
(c) The same holds for W , Mprod and M w (Definition 3.1).
This makes precise Hänni’s heading “input encoding doesn’t matter much” (Hänni, 2026b).
The constant is not small for complex encodings, which can change losses by up to that much
(only the upper bound is proved); non-computable encodings are not covered.
Theorem 2.21 (Coding-theorem bounds). [proved] foundations Thm 6.3 (= history Lemma 1.1, examples Lemma
1.1, context Prop 5.4(1)) For every data set D: (a) LSFI (D) ≥ K(D|T | T )−O(1) for every T ⊆ dom D;
in particular MF (D) ≤ 2−K(D|dom D)+O(1) , and MF (D) ≤ 2−K(y|x)+O(1) for every (x, y) ∈ D; (b)
P
LSFI (D) ≤ min{KF (D),
(x,y)∈D K(y | x)} + O(1) ≤ K(dom D) + K(D | dom D) + O(1), with
KF the shortest consistent program (Definition 3.1); (c) LSFI ({(x, y)}) = K(y | x) ± O(1).
Example 2.22 (Swap data). [proved; computed]
foundations Ex 6.4; checks c9(c), r8 For distinct x1 , x2 ∈ {0, 1}d let D := {(x1 , x2 ), (x2 , x1 )}. Then K(D |
dom D) = O(1), but LSFI (D) ≥ max{K(x2 | x1 ), K(x1 | x2 )} − O(1). Conversely LSFI (D) ≤
d+O(1) for every pair: the machine F (x) := x⊕ω1:|x| has complexity O(1) and extends D exactly
11

when ω1:d = x1 ⊕ x2 , with probability 2−d (c9(c)). For every x1 some x2 has K(x2 | x1 ) ≥ d, and
then LSFI (D) = d ± O(1); also KF (D) ≤ d + 2 log d + O(1).
This is not a separation between SFI and history-aware prediction: a history-aware predictor
shown x1 first must predict x2 from x1 alone and pays K(x2 | x1 ) − O(1) bits. Such separations
are Theorems 5.3, 5.4 and 6.3.
Open question. A two-sided characterisation of LSFI (D) up to O(1) by standard complexities,
other than the tautology LSFI (D) = minν [K(ν) − log ν(D)] ± O(1) over machine laws ν.
Proposition 2.23 (Sequence induction is SFI on initial segments). [proved] foundations Prop 7.1 Let
X = N, {0, 1} ⊆ Y , and M Solomonoff’s universal monotone semimeasure (Zvonkin and Levin,
1970). For x ∈ {0, 1}n put Dx := {(i, xi+1 ) : i < n}. Then 2−O(1) M (x) ≤ MF (Dx ) ≤ 2O(1) M (x).
So SFI generalises Solomonoff induction, and is in turn dominated by universal prediction that
sees the history (Lemma 5.2 and Corollary 5.18).
Proposition 2.24 (Computability). [proved] foundations Prop 7.3 (a) MF (D) is lsc uniformly in D,
and positive. (b) MF is not computable. (c) Neither MF (y | D, x) nor M̄F (y | D, x) is computable
as a function of (D, x, y). (d) Both are computable relative to the halting problem ∅′ .
(c) is diagonal: a computable f that always takes a label of conditional below 0.6 would have
MF (Dn ) < 0.6n . So SFI is an ideal reference, not an algorithm (computable versions: Section 8).
Open question. The exact arithmetical level of the conditionals; for Solomonoff’s M see Leike
and Hutter (2015b) (not checked against the source).

3

Kolmogorov and semimeasure versions

Hänni’s draft has a section headed “Equivalence between Kolmogorov version and universal
semimeasure version”, whose whole text is “need coding theorem” (Hänni, 2026b). He later
asked that the paper “include an equivalence between the kolmogorov complexity version and the
universal semimeasure version, if these turn out to be equivalent (if they don’t, then it should
explain that)” (Hänni, 2026c). They are not equivalent: Table 2 summarises what is equal, what
differs and by how much, and Remark 3.14 answers in plain words. Proofs are in Appendix B.

3.1

The variants

Definition 3.1 (Variants).

foundations Def 4.1 Let (Pi ) be an effective enumeration of all lsc conditional semimeasures (it exists

by trimming each P (· | x) as in Theorem 2.12).

• Program mixture (deterministic programs):

W (D) := p∈dom V 2−|p| 1[φV (p) ⊇ D] = e m(e) 1[φe ⊇ D].
• Kolmogorov version (single shortest program): KF (D) := min{|p| : p ∈ dom V, φV (p) ⊇
D} = min{K(e) : φe ⊇ D}.
• Product mixture (private randomness per input, on data sets):

• Per-query mixture (private randomness per query, on sequences): Mpq (y1:n ∥x1:n ) :=
P

i m(i) t≤n Pi (yt | xt ), for every finite sequence of pairs, consistent or not.
W reads Hänni’s random program as a deterministic program; MF lets it read random bits.
W (∅), Mprod (∅), Mpq (ε∥ε) are below 1, which changes losses by a constant only. W ’s programs
delimit themselves before reading the input, so W is a mixture of fixed functions (Remark B.1).
Remark 3.2 (Operator induction). [known; unverified; equality on distinct inputs proved] foundations Def

4.1, claim F36; context §6 item 1; history Prop 4.9 Solomonoff’s third kind of induction takes “an unordered

set of ordered pairs” and predicts the second element of a new pair from the first (Solomonoff,
P
Q
2008) (abstract only seen). Secondary sources write its prediction as j aj i≤n Oj (Ai | Qi ), the
product running over all question–answer pairs, repeats included (Özkural, 2017) (search snippet
only). On that description operator induction is Mpq : equal to Mprod (Dn ) on distinct inputs,
different with repeats, where Mprod gives a conflicting label probability 0 and Mpq allows fresh
noise (Proposition 4.7). So operator induction and Mprod coincide only on distinct inputs.
Remark 3.3 (Where the randomness lives).
foundations Rem 4.11 Nowhere in W and KF (fixed functions); privately per input in Mprod ; privately
per query in Mpq (a repeated input is resampled); on one tape shared by all inputs in MF , so
one hypothesis may correlate labels across inputs, as F (x) = ω|x|+1 does. Every mixture also
correlates labels through its posterior; the classes still differ by more than constants.

Proposition 3.7 (Shared randomness is not a mixture of private randomness). [proved; counting

Proposition 3.6. A lower bound on the rate is open.

3.3

No coding theorem for function programs

For strings, shortest description and algorithmic probability agree up to a constant (the coding
theorem). For functions they do not.
Theorem 3.9 (No coding theorem for function programs). [proved; arithmetic and covering numbers
computed] foundations Thm 4.7 (= its referee’s Prop A); checks c8(a), c8(b), r11 Let {0, 1} ⊆ Y and d ≥ 2. There
are distinct x1 , x2 ∈ {0, 1}d such that D := {(x1 , 0), (x2 , 1)} satisfies
(i) KF (D) ≥ log d − 2;

(ii) − log W (D) ≤ 2 + K(⌈log(d + 1)⌉) + c;

(iii) LSFI (D) ≤ 2 + c,

with c independent of d. Hence KF (D) + log W (D) ≥ log d − K(⌈log(d + 1)⌉) − c − 4 → ∞, and
on these data sets − log W (D) ≤ log KF (D) + O(log log KF (D)).
(i) is the colouring argument with ℓ = ℓd , (ii) is Proposition 3.6(b), (iii) the fair-coin product
law. So a c.e. set of programs, {e : φe ⊇ D}, can have algorithmic probability 2−O(log log d) while
its simplest member has complexity log d − O(1) (related literature: Appendix B.5).
Corollary 3.10 (Two-point rate). [proved]
foundations Cor 4.8 Let ρK (d) be the largest value of KF (D) − LSFI (D) over two-point data sets D
with binary labels and d-bit inputs. Then log d − O(1) ≤ ρK (d) ≤ log d + K(⌈log(d + 2)⌉) + O(1) =
log d + O(log log d).
Proposition 3.11 (Covering bound for the single shortest program). [proved; computed] foundations
Prop 4.9 (its referee’s Prop A3, sharpened); checks c8(c,d), r11(d) For every D with labels in {0, 1}, |D| = k and
dom D ⊆ {0, 1}≤d ,
KF (D) ≤ k + log k(d + 2) + K ⌈log(d + 2)⌉, k + O(1).


14



Consequently, with δD := k − K(D | dom D) (the deficiency of randomness of the labels given the
inputs), KF (D) ≤ LSFI (D) + δD + log(k(d + 2)) + K(⌈log(d + 2)⌉, k) + O(1).
(About 2k k(d + 2) ln 2 random truth tables cover all such data sets; a program indexes the
first covering family.) So a logarithmic cost is real for the single shortest program, while W pays
no factor depending on |D|.
Remark 3.12 (Witnesses of large gaps cannot be computable). [proved] foundations Rem 4.10 If k 7→ Dk
is computable, then KF (Dk ) ≤ K(k) + O(1), so the five quantities of Theorem 3.4, all between
2−KF (Dk )−O(1) and 1, agree on Dk up to K(k) + O(1) bits; if Dd has d-bit inputs and d 7→ Dd
is computable, they agree up to O(1). So the witnesses of Proposition 3.5 and Theorem 3.9 and
Theorem 2.13(iii) are non-constructive by necessity; Theorem 2.13(iv) is an explicit family with
a linear gap on average.
Open question. Is there a computable family k 7→ Dk along which some gap of Table 2 tends
to infinity (necessarily no faster than K(k))?

3.4

Summary and meaning for prediction

Remark 3.13 (What the gaps mean for prediction). [proved] foundations §4 “What the differences mean
for prediction” (1) Gaps are cumulative losses: by Proposition 2.16, a gap of g bits between two of
W, Mprod , MF , M w on D is a difference of g bits in total log loss on D in every order (semimeasure
convention, up to the constants log W (∅), log Mprod (∅)); single steps can differ more or less. (2)
Random tape or not: W predicts as well as SFI up to K pad (L1/2 (MF , D)) + O(1) bits in total,
and up to K(⌈log(d + 1)⌉) + O(1) bits on fair-coin labels however many are predicted. So whether
Hänni’s random program has a random tape matters little, unless SFI’s explanation of the data
uses a number of tape bits with no simple bound; likewise private versus shared randomness, as
Mprod lies between W and MF . (3) The shortest program: KF (D) is a description length (MDL
with one hypothesis; the Occam learner of Proposition 4.15), not a predictor’s loss. It can exceed
SFI’s loss by log d − O(1) bits on two points and by about log(k(d + 2)) on k incompressible binary
labels; for deterministic targets it has SFI’s learning rate (Proposition 4.15). (4) M w can beat MF
by linearly many bits on data no random function produces (blocks of ν△ ), but has no posterior
over functions. (5) For a deterministic computable f all five are at least 2−K(f )−O(1) on its data,
so the basic learning bounds (Theorem 4.1(b), Corollary 4.4, Theorem 4.5(a)) hold for all. (6)
Fresh label noise with repeated inputs separates Mpq from all set-based objects: finite against
infinite loss (Propositions 4.6 and 4.7). (7) On a single query SFI is the conditional universal
distribution, 2−K(y|x)±O(1) ; it commits to where the output ends, unlike sequence induction on an
undelimited output, and with an end marker the two agree up to constants (Proposition B.4).
Remark 3.14 (Answer to Hänni’s request). [proved] foundations §4 “Answer to the question in Hänni’s draft”;

claims F16, F17 There are three objects, and no two of them agree up to a constant.

• The single shortest program KF falls short of MF by log d ± O(log log d) bits at worst on two
points with d-bit inputs (Corollary 3.10): one program must be chosen, and short programs
cannot separate every pair of inputs, while a random tape separates any two at constant
cost.
• The program mixture W falls short of MF unboundedly (Proposition 3.5), but only by the
cost of naming a padded tape length, since the 2L programs storing L-bit tape prefixes
together weigh 2−K(L) (Proposition 3.6).

The coding theorem, which Hänni’s draft names as the tool, gives agreement of all five versions
up to K(dom D) + O(1) bits (Theorem 3.4), not up to a constant. For deterministic computable
targets all have loss at most K(f ) + O(1) on the data of f .

and K pad (2d+1 ) + O(1) against fair coins; L1/2 is not computably bounded in (d, |D|). Also open:

4

Learning theorems: SFI as supervised learning

Hänni describes SFI as “just ideal supervised learning” (Hänni, 2026c). This section states the
guarantees behind that description and where they stop. Throughout, Dn = {(xt , yt ) : t ≤
n} and LSFI (Dn ) = − log MF (Dn ) is SFI’s cumulative log loss in the semimeasure convention,
independent of order and repetitions and infinite on inconsistent sequences (Proposition 2.16);
upper bounds in it also hold normalised (Proposition 2.17). We write µ for the input distribution,
c for a machine constant, and Lν (D) := − log ν(D). For an lsc conditional semimeasure P
(Proposition 2.10(a)), K(P ) is the least K(i) over its indices in the enumeration of Definition 3.1.
For a partial computable φ, K(φ) is the least K(e) with φe = φ.

4.1

Dominance

Theorem 4.1 (dominance). [proved] foundations Thm 6.1 Let ν be a machine law (Definition 2.7).
Then MF (D) ≥ 2−K(ν) ν(D) for every data set D. Hence for every consistent sequence, however
its inputs are chosen (in advance, adversarially, adaptively, non-computably),
LSFI (Dn ) ≤ − log ν(Dn ) + K(ν).

(2)

In particular: (a) x-only samplers: for every lsc conditional semimeasure P , LSFI (Dn ) ≤
P
t≤n − log P (yt | xt ) + K(P ) + c, where the right side counts a repeated input each time it
appears and the left side once; (b) deterministic programs: if yt = φ(xt ) for all t, with φ
partial computable, then LSFI (Dn ) ≤ K(φ) + c; (c) weak comparators: for every lsc weak
semimeasure ν (Definition 2.2), LSFI (Dn ) ≤ − log ν(Dn ) + K(ν) + K(dom Dn ) + c. The
term K(dom Dn ) cannot be replaced by a constant (semimeasure convention): on the 2k-point
data sets of Theorem 2.13(iii), LSFI exceeds − log M w by at least k log 23 − K(k) − c, while
K(dom D) ≤ k log 3 + K(k) + c.
Proof. Theorem 2.9 gives MF ≥ 2−K(ν) ν, and Proposition 2.16 gives LSFI (Dn ) = − log MF (Dn )
Q
in every order. (a) The product law νP (D) = (x,y)∈D P (y | x) is a machine law with K(νP ) ≤
K(P ) + c (Proposition 2.10(a)); it counts each input once, and the extra factors of repeats are
at most 1. (b) Proposition 2.10(b). (c) Theorem 2.13(ii),(iii); each domain of that family is
determined by k and a vector in {1, 2, 3}k .
Taking expectations gives the bound under every data distribution. Part (a) is Hänni’s “Thm
1 (template)” for computably samplable x-only predictors (Hänni, 2026b): each “loses to ‘the
solomonoff inductor for C’” by “at most a constant (depending on P but not the distribution)”.
It holds in his setting of “input-output pairs from a function” (consistent sequences), with randomness read once per input, as his remark on “different bits of randomness . . . for sampling on
different inputs” suggests.
Remark 4.2 (scope of dominance). [proved; open part] foundations §6.1, F20–F23 (i) The comparators
include all computable laws of random total functions, for every countable Y (Proposition 2.10
and Lemma A.2). (ii) The label sequence must be consistent: if an input reappears with another
label, LSFI = ∞, while a sampler with fresh randomness per query has finite loss. Randomness
per input is the right reading for data from a function (Remark 2.11); per query it gives Mpq
(Proposition 4.7). (iii) For general lsc laws of random partial functions the statement is open
(Section 2.3).

Proof idea. Given the past, yt has law ν(· | Dt−1 , xt ), so the expected log ratio at step t is dt ;
the ratios telescope to log(ν(Dn )/MF (Dn )) ≤ K(ν) (Theorem 4.1; integrability in Appendix C.1).

Corollary 4.14 (per-step in-distribution error). [proved; computed (context c7)] context Cor 2.17 Let µ
be any distribution on X, f partial computable with µ(dom f ) = 1, x1 , . . . , xn , x∗ i.i.d. from µ,

For every ε ∈ (0, 1), Ee(x∗ ) ≤ ε + 2K(f )+c e−εn ; hence Ee(x∗ ) ≤ ((K(f ) + c) ln 2 + ln n + 1)/n.

4.5

Comparison, and the sense of “ideal”

Proposition 4.15 (Occam bound in description-length form). [proved (standard); references known,
unverified] foundations §6.6; F32 Let xt be i.i.d. from µ, φ partial computable with µ(dom φ) = 1,
yt = φ(xt ). With probability at least 1 − δ, every program p ∈ dom V consistent with Dn has
µ{x : φV (p) (x) ̸= φ(x)} ≤ (|p| ln 2 + ln(1/δ))/n, where “̸=” includes “undefined”. So the shortest
consistent program, of length KF (Dn ) ≤ K(φ), has 0–1 risk at most (K(φ) ln 2 + ln(1/δ))/n.
So for deterministic targets SFI (Theorem 4.5(a)) and the Kolmogorov version learn at the
same rate, though their code lengths can differ (Theorem 3.9). Appendix C.7 compares with
Occam, PAC-Bayes, resolvability and expert-advice bounds (Blumer et al., 1987; McAllester,
1999; Barron and Cover, 1991; Vovk, 1990; Cesa-Bianchi and Lugosi, 2006).
Remark 4.16 (in what sense SFI is ideal supervised learning). [remark (each item cited)] foundations
(1) SFI is the Bayes predictor for the prior “a random lower-semicomputable
labeller”, machine laws weighted by m(i) ≥ 2−K(i) (Theorem 2.9), and it is universal for that
class pathwise (Theorem 4.1). (2) With i.i.d. inputs from any distribution and computable labels, the averaged predictor has expected log and 0–1 risk at most (K(φ) + c)/n; with frozen
noise, or fresh noise on stamped inputs or for Mpq , the cumulative bound K(P ) + c holds (Theorem 4.5 and Proposition 4.7). This is the Occam rate with K for log |H|, without a union
bound or confidence parameter. (3) Inputs are never predicted, so the input distribution may
be arbitrary at no cost (chosen inputs: Proposition C.1; positive examples and interventions:
Remarks C.5 and C.6). Limits:
loss: the guarantees are for log loss on labels, and 0–1 bounds
p
follow (K/n without noise, K/n excess with noise); averaged predictor: Proposition 4.8 and Remark 4.9; function semantics: Proposition 4.6; computability: Proposition 2.24, and Section 8 for
computable versions; constants: machine constants, and K(α, β) for encodings (Theorem 2.20);
§6.7 Established:

21

distribution shift: Section 7; comparator class: simple history-aware predictors have loss 0 where
SFI’s loss is unbounded (Theorems 5.3, 5.4 and 6.3; the swap data of Example 2.22 do not show
this), and SFIhist removes this limit (Corollary 5.18).
Remark 4.17 (prior work on the framing). [known; unverified] foundations §6.6 last item; history §4.6 item 2 A
table-of-contents snippet shows a section “Supervised Learning from Examples (EX)” in Hutter
(2007), and the abstracts of Hutter (2000) and of that chapter list supervised learning among
the AIXI model’s problem classes; Hutter (2005) is the book-length treatment. We have not read
these sections; the universal mixture as ideal learner from examples is prior work in substance.

5

History-aware prediction

SFI sees the past only as a set: its hypotheses answer each query without seeing the other inputs, and its loss LSFI (Dn ) in the semimeasure convention ignores the order of the data (Proposition 2.16). Hänni’s notes (Hänni, 2026b) give two examples where seeing the history helps without
bound, and a dominance theorem (“Thm 1”) for simple reveal orders; his request conjectures that
SFI whose hypotheses see the list of previous inputs “just becomes as good as SI” (Hänni, 2026c).
We prove the examples, decide Thm 1 in three readings (some cases stay open), and prove the
conjecture in a precise form. Lower bounds on LSFI are in the semimeasure convention unless
stated; cω depends on the machines and on a Martin-Löf random ω. Proofs and some secondary
statements are in Appendix D.

5.1

History-aware predictors

Definition 5.1 (History-aware predictors). history §1.3, Lemma 1.0; foundations Prop 7.2 A history-aware
predictor P gives sub-probabilities P (y | h, x); its loss is LP := − log P (y1:n ∥x1:n ), P (y1:n ∥x1:n ) :=
Q
i≤n P (yi | hi−1 , xi ). A computable sampler is a probabilistic machine that, given (h, x), reads
random bits and halts with a label or diverges (defective if with positive probability); P (y | h, x)
is its probability of output y, lsc in the arguments. An input sampler (reveal law) ∆ outputs
an input given h. A chronological semimeasure is a map ρ(y1:n ∥x1:n ) ≥ 0 with ρ(ϵ∥ϵ) ≤ 1 and
P
y ρ(y1:n y∥x1:n x) ≤ ρ(y1:n ∥x1:n ) for all x; Lρ := − log ρ(y1:n ∥x1:n ).
These are Hutter’s chronological semimeasures, with inputs as his actions and labels as his
percepts (Hutter, 2000, 2005). Hänni’s class C is the computable samplers; one that halts with
P
probability 1 has computable probabilities (1 − y′ ̸=y P (y ′ | h, x) is upper semicomputable).
Lemma 5.2 (SFI is a chronological semimeasure). [proved] history Lemma 1.2; foundations Prop 7.2(b),(c)
ρF (y1:n ∥x1:n ) := U{ω : fω (xi ) = yi ∀i ≤ n} is an lsc chronological semimeasure, equal to MF (Dn )
on consistent sequences and 0 otherwise. Hence the loss LH of SFIhist (Definition 5.16) satisfies
LH ≤ LSFI (Dn ) + c in every order. The lsc chronological semimeasures that depend only on Dn
and vanish on inconsistent sequences are exactly the lsc weak function semimeasures.

5.2

Hänni’s counterexamples

Hänni’s notes reveal “a deterministic sequence of increasingly long random strings” as
(x1 , 0), (x2 , x1 ), . . . and write: “I’m pretty sure Solomonoff induction gets unbounded loss
on this sequence. I don’t actually have a proof”; a “cleaner construction” gives “0 to everything
except one string you’ve skipped among all strings of a certain length” (Hänni, 2026b). Both
claims hold. (We take ϵ as the chain’s first label.)

Theorem 5.3 (Hänni’s chain). [proved] history Thm 2.1 Let x1 , . . . , xn (n ≥ 2) be distinct nonempty

Proposition 5.5 (Every order-free predictor pays). [proved] history Prop 2.3 Let ν be a weak function
P

chains D(x, z) := {(x, z1 ), (z1 , z2 ), . . . , (zk , ϵ)} (k ≥ 0) of distinct nonempty strings zi ̸= x. (b) If

every ν, with independent uniform blocks, E[− log ν(Dn )] ≥ ℓ̄n−1 and E[− log ν(D(ℓ) )] ≥ ℓ(ℓ+1)/2.
So every predictor that sees the past as a set and is order-independent pays the cost (Hänni’s
predictors are not order-independent). The normalised M̄F is not order-free, but its normaliser is
at least 2−K(B)−c times the probability that a total machine B extends the data (Lemma D.2). For
the chain, the broom Bj reads blocks v1 , . . . , vj of lengths ℓ1 , . . . , ℓj , sets f (v1 ) := ϵ, f (vi ) := vi−1 ,
and sends every other input to vj ; it extends Dj+1 with probability 2−ℓ̄j at a cost of K(j) + c bits
(as in Proposition 6.6).
Proposition 5.6 (Normalised SFI on both examples). [proved; finite proxy computed]
history Prop 2.4, Lemma 2.4a; check c9 (a) In the setting of Theorem 5.3(b), − log M̄F (yt | Dt−1 , xt ) ≥
ℓt−1 − K(ℓt ) − K(t − 2) − cω for t ≥ 2; for ℓi = i, L̄SFI ≥ n(n − 1)/2 − n(4 log n + cω ). With
independent uniform blocks the expected normalised loss at step t is at least ℓt−1 − K(t − 2) − c, so
EL̄SFI ≥ n(n−1)/2−n(2 log n+c) for ℓi = i. (b) In the setting of Theorem 5.4(c), the normalised
loss at sℓ is at least ℓ − 2K(ℓ) − cω , so L̄SFI ≥ ℓ(ℓ + 1)/2 − ℓ(4 log ℓ + cω ) after block ℓ ≥ 2; with
independent uniform sj , at least ℓ − K(ℓ) − c in expectation.
The method needs more information per step than the O(log t) bits of the broom, so it gives
nothing for Proposition 5.19 and Theorem 5.26.

5.3

Hänni’s Theorem 1

Hänni’s Thm 1: if f is drawn and the inputs are drawn from ∆, “each predictor P in C can
only beat solomonoff function induction in expected log loss by at most the complexity C∆ of
sampling from the reveal sequence distribution ∆ plus a constant CP ”, in the case where “the
reveal sequence distribution is simple to sample from conditional on any particular choice of
function f ”; a preceding remark says it fails if one puts “the randomization inside the reveal
sequence distribution” of the chain (Hänni, 2026b).
Definition 5.7 (Three readings). history §3 Let yi = f (xi ). Reading A: one reveal law ∆(xi | hi−1 )
that does not see f (private randomness and memory allowed), f arbitrary. Reading C : for each
f a reveal law ∆f (x | h) that sees only the history, with C∆ := K(∆f ) as a standalone sampler;
A is the case ∆f = ∆. Reading B: xi ∼ ∆(· | f, hi−1 ), with C∆ the complexity of ∆ relative to
an oracle for f .
Hänni’s text fixes Reading C: his example of failure has C∆ = O(1) in Reading B but is
excluded in Reading C (Theorem 5.12), and his sketch simulates ∆ in a function machine, which
P
cannot see f . Let I(f → x1:n ) := i≤n I(f ; xi | hi−1 ) (directed information). A reveal law
that does not see f may keep private memory; it enters only through its reveal-sequence law
∆(x1:n ∥y1:n−1 ), and I(f → x) = 0 (Lemma D.3).
Proposition 5.8 (The proof sketch fails). [computed; proved] history Prop 3.1; check c1 Let X = {a, b},
f (a) = f (b) = β for a uniform bit β, and let ∆ show a, then b. The predictor with y1 uniform
and y2 = y1 has loss 1 bit; the simulator of the proof sketch, run with separate randomness for
each input, has loss 2 bits.
The sketch “internally simulates” ∆ and P and then uses “jensen’s inequality” (Hänni, 2026b).
A simulator with private randomness per input loses the cross-input correlations that P uses, and
the Jensen step averages over simulated histories, whose labels come from P , not f . (SFI itself
pays at most 1 + c bits here.) One simulation shared by all inputs works, at the price of the reveal
sequence’s log loss:
Theorem 5.9 (Shared-randomness simulator). [proved] history Thm 3.2 For computable samplers ∆
(inputs) and P (labels), possibly defective and with private memory, and every consistent sequence,
MF (Dn ) ≥ 2−K(∆,P )−c ∆(x1:n ∥y1:n−1 )P (y1:n ∥x1:n ); so LSFI (Dn ) ≤ LP + L∆ + K(∆) + K(P ) + c
with L∆ := − log ∆(x1:n ∥y1:n−1 ). For distinct inputs the product may be summed over all orders
of Dn .
Theorem 5.10 (Bayes form). [proved] history Thm 3.5; checks c2, r1 Let f ∼ ν, a computable random
function (a machine law of a random total function, Lemma A.2), and let the inputs see f only
through revealed labels: xi = seli (Γ, hi−1 ), arbitrary maps, Γ independent of f . Then the Bayes
predictor ν(y | Dh , x) (Dh the data of h; Definition 2.1) is the true conditional law, with loss
− log ν(Dn ), and LSFI (Dn ) ≤ − log ν(Dn ) + K(ν) + c on every path. So ELSFI (Dn ) ≤ ELP +
K(ν) + c for every predictor P , computable or not.
Theorem 5.11 (Directed information). [proved; identity computed] history Thm 3.6; checks c2, r1 Let
f ∼ ν, let the inputs have any joint law with f , yi = f (xi ), finite alphabets up to time n, and
P
LP := − i≤n log P(yi | hi−1 , xi ). (a) For any law ν, E[− log ν(Dn )] − ELP = I(f → x1:n ) −
E KL(P(f ∈ · | hn )∥ν(f ∈ · | f ⊇ Dn )) ≤ I(f → x1:n ). (b) For computable ν and every P ,
ELSFI (Dn ) ≤ ELP + I(f → x1:n ) + K(ν) + c.

Theorem 5.12 (Hänni’s randomized chain; Reading B is false). [proved; refutes Reading B] history
Thm 3.7; check r1 Let xi be independent and uniform on {0, 1}i , f (xi+1 ) := xi and f := ϵ elsewhere;

This is Hänni’s own example, and his remark is correct. It refutes only Reading B: in Reading

For arbitrary f assume (A1) Y is finite and (A2) a computable map j 7→ Aj to finite, listed
subsets of X, of complexity K(A), has all inputs up to step n in An . Let Cdir (∆, n) := supν Iν (f →
x1:n ) over laws ν on Y An (0 if ∆ does not see f ).
Theorem 5.13 (Minimax theorem). [(a) proved; (b) proved for reveal laws that do not see f , proof sketch
for those that do; computed] history Thm 3.8; checks c3, c8, r2 Fix n, assume (A1), (A2), and let the reveal
law see the history (with private memory) and possibly f |An . Assume that P (y | h, x) and the
reveal-sequence law have (a) rational values computable from their arguments, or (b) computable
real values (this includes all computable samplers that halt with probability 1; Lemma D.3). Then
for every f : An → Y , and on average over any law of f independent of the reveal law’s private
randomness, Ex|f LSFI (Dn ) ≤ Ex|f LP + Cdir (∆, n) + K(P ) + K(∆) + K(n) + K(A) + c.
Proof idea. Sion’s theorem (Sion, 1958) and Theorem 5.11(a) give a law on Y An within Cdir +2
bits of P for every f , found by a search computable from (P, ∆, n, A). For reveal laws that see f ,
case (b) needs Cdir to within 1, not written out.
Corollary 5.14 (Hänni’s Theorem 1 in Reading C). [proved under the stated hypotheses] history Cor 3.9
(a) Under (A1), (A2), with P and every ∆f of computable real values (e.g. samplers halting with
probability 1, private memory allowed): Ex|f LSFI (Dn ) ≤ Ex|f LP +K(P )+K(∆f )+K(n)+K(A)+c
for every f ; for random f , with Ef K(∆f ). (b) If xi = δf (hi−1 ) with δf computable: for every
f , sampler P (defective allowed) and n, LSFI (Dn ) ≤ LP + K(δf ) + K(P ) + c on every path
(Corollary D.4). (c) For all samplers P, ∆f , with no finiteness assumption, Ex|f LSFI ≤ Ex|f LP +
Hf (x1:n ) + K(∆f ) + K(P ) + c, Hf the entropy of the reveal sequence given f .
So Thm 1 is true in Hänni’s reading, with an extra K(n) + K(A) for random reveal laws;
only the proof sketch fails. Open cases are listed in Remark D.6; no pathwise version with K(∆)
alone holds (Proposition D.7). The Shannon form of his mutual-information question holds: if
the reveal law is indexed by a random Θ and does not see f , then I(f → x) ≤ I(f ; Θ), with
equality in the randomized chain (Proposition D.8). The algorithmic form is untested:
Conjecture 5.15 (Algorithmic form in Reading C). [conjecture (untested)]
history Conj 3.11; Q8 With I(a : b) := K(b) − K(b | a) and the hypotheses of Corollary 5.14(a):
Ex|f LSFI ≤ Ex|f LP + I(∆f : f |An ) + K(P ) + O(log K(∆f )) + K(n) + K(A) + c.

5.4

SFI with access to the previous inputs

Hänni’s request: “if you further give the function oracle access to a list of previous x (but not
previous y), then it just becomes as good as SI” (Hänni, 2026c).
25

Definition 5.16 (SFIhist ).
history §4.1 Let XH be the set of nonempty finite input lists. A history-oracle function machine
is a function machine T on XH (Definition 2.7), queried at time i at x1:i ; it never sees labels
but can recompute its own. Its law MThist (y1:n ∥x1:n ) := U{ω : T ω (x1:i ) = yi ∀i ≤ n} (n ≥ 1;
1 at the root) is an lsc chronological semimeasure (Lemma D.9). SFIhist is SFI over XH : its
H
law is MH := MThist
H , TU the universal function machine for XH (Theorem 2.9), and its loss is
U

LH := − log MH (y1:n ∥x1:n ). We define the universal chronological semimeasure as MH .
Theorem 5.17 (Representation). [proved; construction computed] history Thm 4.2; checks c4, r5 For every
lsc chronological semimeasure ρ there is a history-oracle function machine Tρ with MThist
= ρ at
ρ

The proof allocates dyadic intervals as Zvonkin and Levin (1970) do for sequences. For sequences, Wood et al. (2013) state that machine-based and semimeasure-based universal priors
form the same class (abstract seen only); we have not checked whether Theorem 5.17 appears in
this form in Hutter’s work, and claim no novelty for it.
Corollary 5.18 (Universality of SFIhist ). [proved; identification known (unverified)]
history Cor 4.3; foundations Prop 7.2(a),(d) (a) MH ≥ 2−K(ρ)−c ρ for every lsc chronological semimeasure
ρ; so MH is a universal element of this class and agrees with every other one up to constant
factors. (b) For every lsc history-aware predictor P (e.g. every computable sampler, defective or
not) and every sequence, consistent or not: LH ≤ LP + K(P ) + c. (c) LH ≤ LSFI (Dn ) + c on
every consistent sequence, in every order.
So Hänni’s conjecture holds in this sense. A trimming construction as in Theorem 2.12 gives
the same law up to constants. Agreement with Hutter’s universal chronological semimeasure (Hutter, 2000, 2005) is known, but not checked against the sources (he uses finite alphabets), and is not
used; his “supervised learning from examples” is prior work in substance (Remark 4.17). Oracles
that see the previous pairs, the time i, or the set of previous inputs define SFIpairs , SFIcount (SFI
on time-stamped inputs) and SFIset , with laws Mpairs , Mcount , Mset and losses Lpairs , Lcount , Lset ;
on distinct inputs SFI ⪯ SFIcount ⪯ SFIset ⪯ SFIhist up to constant factors, and Mpairs agrees
with MH up to constant factors (Proposition D.10).
Proposition 5.19 (The hierarchy is strict). [proved; lemma computed] history Prop 4.5; checks c6, r4
Semimeasure convention. (i) Every set S of n ≥ 2 strings has an order such that, with labels
yi = 1[i even], LSFI ≥ n − log(n + 1) − c while Lcount ≤ c. (ii) On the chain of Theorem 5.3(b),
Lcount ≥ ℓ̄n−1 − K(ℓn ) − cω while Lset ≤ c. (iii) Every such S has an order such that, with labels
y1 = ϵ, yi = xi−1 , Lset ≥ log n! − log n − c while LH ≤ c. Single infinite sequences with linearly
growing gaps also exist (Proposition D.11).
In (iii) a tape determines the order backwards from its last input. So the oracle must give
the list, not the set (also Proposition 6.8). Operator induction, read as Mpq , ignores the history:
SFIhist dominates it, and on distinct inputs it pays the lower bounds of Theorems 5.3 and 5.4
(Proposition D.12).
For deterministic programs on XH , the program mixture Whist pays at most K tail (n) :=
P
− log N ≥n 2−K(N ) extra bits against fair-coin labels, and K tail (n) ≤ g(n) + K(g) + c for every
computable nondecreasing unbounded g, while the single shortest program pays more than log n+
log log n extra bits infinitely often (Proposition D.13): as for SFI (Theorem 3.9), the Kolmogorov
and mixture versions differ unboundedly.

So for deterministic input policies, “LH ≤ LyM + O(1)” holds iff M predicts the inputs with
bounded loss. Lattimore et al. (2011) state that “the unnormalised version can fail to predict very

loss of unnormalised M on positions 2, 4, . . . of ω1 ω1 ω2 ω2 · · · is unbounded, and (LHG-odd) the
same on positions 3, 5, . . . of 0ω1 ω1 ω2 ω2 · · · .
Proposition 5.22 (The conditional label loss). [conditional (on LHG transfer); LHG known, unverified
beyond the abstract] history §4.4, Q3 With X = Y = {0, 1} and the identity code: under (LHG-even),
LyM ≤ LH + O(1) fails on some sequence (labels copy inputs); under (LHG-odd), LH ≤ LyM + O(1)
fails on some sequence (inputs copy the previous label, Proposition 5.21). For the code s̄ = 1|s| 0s
the hypotheses must also transfer to framed blocks.
This does not contradict Corollary 5.18: M ’s conditionals need not be lsc. For normalised M
both directions are open.
Remark 5.23 (Verdict on Hänni’s conjecture). [remark (cited items)] history §7 “H4” With the list of
previous inputs SFI becomes the universal chronological semimeasure (Corollary 5.18); the set is
not enough (Proposition 5.19(iii)). “As good as SI” holds against Solomonoff induction’s joint
loss (Theorem 5.20(a)); against its conditional label loss the difference is M ’s loss on the inputs
(Proposition 5.21), and for unnormalised M it fails both ways, conditionally on Proposition 5.22;
the normalised case is open.

5.6

What SFI can and cannot exploit

For D with distinct inputs let Dσ be D in order σ, xσ its input list, and K pred (x1:n ) := min{|p| :
V (p, xi ) = ⟨x1 , . . . , xi−1 ⟩ ∀i ≤ n}, the complexity of recovering each input’s predecessors from the
input.
Theorem 5.24 (Sandwich). [proved]
history Thm 5.2 (with Prop 5.1) For every data set D: maxσ LH (Dσ ) − c ≤ LSFI (D) ≤ minσ ′ [LH (Dσ ′ ) +
K pred (xσ′ )] + c.
Corollary 5.25 (Initial segments). [proved] history Cor 5.3 If dom D = {e1 , . . . , ek } for a computable
injective sequence e, then |LSFI (D) − LH (D in e-order)| ≤ K(e) + c, and the e-order is the worst
for SFIhist up to K(e) + c.
27

This covers the skipped strings and the full cyclic permutation (K(e) ≤ K(d) + c). The K pred
term can be needed beyond order information: identify {0, 1}q with GF(2q ) (polynomial basis
modulo the lexicographically least irreducible polynomial) and let Dabr := {(a, r), (b, r + ab)},
a ̸= b.
Theorem 5.26 (A GF(2q ) example). [proved; computed] history Thm 5.4, Lemma 5.5; checks c5, r3, s4 (a) In
both orders LH (Dabr ) ≤ q+c, with c independent of q. (b) LSFI (Dabr ) ≥ K(a, b, r | q)−3q/2−1−c;
so for all but a 21−k fraction of triples, LSFI (Dabr ) ≥ 3q/2 − k − c ≥ maxσ LH (Dσ ) + q/2 − k − c′ .
(c) For uniform (a, b, r), a ̸= b, ELSFI (Dabr ) ≥ 3q/2 − 2 for every q ≥ 1. These lower bounds are
in the semimeasure convention; for L̄SFI they are open.
SFIhist predicts y2 = y1 + x1 x2 ; SFI’s hypotheses never see the first input. The key step is a
character-sum bound (Lemma D.15). A direct computation (code in the accompanying repository)
gives the optimum over all laws of random functions, minw E[− log w(Dabr )] = 2.708 bits for q = 2
and 4.348 for q = 3, against q for SFIhist .
Theorem 5.27 (Expected gap). [proved]
history Thm 5.6 Let f ∼ ν computable and the inputs drawn by any kernel ∆(· | f, hi−1 ), with finite
alphabets up to time n. Then −c ≤ ELSFI (Dn ) − ELH ≤ I(f → x1:n ) + K(ν) + c, and the gap is
at most K(ν) + c if the input choice does not see f .
The upper bound is attained up to n in the randomized chain, but not in general: the GF
triple revealed in random order has I(f → x) ≈ 2q but a gap of at most q + c (and fails Reading
B: q bits for SFIhist , ELSFI ≥ 3q/2 − 2).
On individual sequences, LSFI (D) ≤ LH (Dσ ) + I(xσ : D) + c, with I(a : b) := K(b) − K(b | a);
the natural strengthening with I(xσ : yσ ) in place of I(xσ : D) is false, by a margin linear in
n (Proposition D.16). Informally, shared randomness lets a label of SFI correlate with a guess
about the other inputs, not with the inputs present (Remark D.17).
Open questions. (i) The open cases of Reading C (Remark D.6). (ii) Do (LHG-even), (LHGodd) hold? How does LH compare with normalised M ’s label loss? (iii) Normalised SFI on
= LH + Lxin ± O(1) (“≤” is proved)?
Propositions 5.19 and D.11 and Theorem 5.26. (iv) Is Ljoint
M
(v) The growth of maxG N (G) in Lemma D.15 (local search suggests about 21.35q ; an observation
only). (vi) Conjecture 5.15. (vii) Which individual-sequence quantity measures LSFI (D)−LH (Dσ )
tightly? (viii) The exact rate in Proposition D.13(d).

6

Worked examples

Hänni (2026c) proposed two examples in which SFI “does much worse than SI”: a random cyclic
permutation revealed backwards along its cycle, and a coin-flip example due to Sam Eisenstat,
which Hänni reported with an argument from his e-mail to Eisenstat. We work both out and
add examples in which SFI does well (his out-of-distribution examples are in Section 7). Losses
are in the semimeasure convention, LSFI (Dn ) = − log MF (Dn ) in every order (Proposition 2.16),
unless stated otherwise; upper bounds also hold for the normalised predictor M̄F , lower bounds
only where we say so (Remark 2.18). c is a machine constant, j ≥ 0 a confidence parameter, and
a finite function is identified with its graph. Proofs are in Appendix E.

6.2

The cyclic permutation

Definition 6.2 (the cyclic permutation). examples §2.1; Hänni’s request Fix d ≥ 1, N := 2d and
a cyclic permutation f of {0, 1}d (a single N -cycle). Put x0 := 0d and xk := f −1 (xk−1 ) for
1 ≤ k ≤ N . The k-th revealed pair is (xk , xk−1 ), and Dk := {(xi , xi−1 ) : i ≤ k}. We write
(a)k := a(a − 1) · · · (a − k + 1), (a)0 := 1.
Hänni expected that this “will have very low loss under SI but high loss under SFI” (Hänni,
2026c). The map f 7→ (x1 , . . . , xN −1 ) is a bijection onto the orderings of the nonzero strings (its
inverse closes the cycle 0d 7→ xN −1 7→ · · · 7→ x1 7→ 0d ), so for uniform f the ordering is uniform.
For k < N , Dk is a chain xk → · · · → x1 → 0d of distinct nonzero strings. The labels add nothing
to the inputs (yk = xk−1 ): all of f is in the order.
Theorem 6.3 (the full cycle). [proved; computed (examples e3, e6(d))]

examples Thm 2.1 Semimeasure convention. (a) For every total f : {0, 1}d → {0, 1}d , |LSFI (f )−K(f |
d)| ≤ c. (b) For every cyclic permutation f of {0, 1}d , LSFI (f ) ≤ log((N − 1)!) + c. (c) For
uniform cyclic f , P[LSFI (f ) < log((N − 1)!) − j − c] < 2−j , and ELSFI (f ) ≥ log((N − 1)!) − c.
1
1
(d) log((N − 1)!) = (d − log e)N − d2 + 12 log(2π) + ϵN with (12N +1)
ln 2 < ϵN < 12N ln 2 . The same

bounds hold for LSFI (DN −1 ).

So SFI pays d − log e ≈ d − 1.443 bits per pair over the cycle (Table 3). In (a) one function
machine reads a single program for the whole table from the shared tape; in (b) one machine draws
a uniform cyclic permutation of each length; (d) is Stirling’s formula with Robbins’ remainder
bounds (Robbins, 1955).
Theorem 6.4 (k steps). [proved; computed (examples e6(a),(b),(c))]

examples Thm 2.3 Semimeasure convention; 1 ≤ k ≤ N − 1. (a) On every chain, LSFI (Dk ) ≤ log(N −

1)k−1 +min{K(k), log(N −k)}+c. (b) For uniform cyclic f , P[LSFI (Dk ) < log(N −1)k−1 −j −c] <
2−j . (c) For uniform cyclic f , with probability at least 1−2−j , LSFI (Dk ) ≥ log(N −1)k−1 −d−j −c
for all k < N at once.
29

So LSFI (Dk ) = log(N − 1)k−1 ± O(K(k)) with high probability: SFI pays about log(N − t + 1)
bits for the label at step t ≥ 2, and the newest input is free up to K(k). The upper bound
uses a broom Bk , a function machine that draws, for each length ℓ, a uniform injective sequence
(v1 , . . . , vk−1 ) of nonzero ℓ-bit strings and maps v1 7→ 0ℓ , vi 7→ vi−1 , and every other input to
vk−1 ; it extends Dk with probability 1/(N − 1)k−1 . It randomises the upper-bound program of
Hänni’s chain example (Theorem 5.3). The lower bounds rest on a depth count (Lemma E.3).
The cost is intrinsic to order-free prediction:
Proposition 6.5 (order-free predictors). [proved; sharper weak case open]
examples Prop 2.4 (general lemma: history Prop 2.3) Let f be uniform cyclic and k < N .

(a) For every law
ν of a random partial function, computable or not (so every machine law, with shared or private
randomness), E[− log ν(Dk )] ≥ log(N − 1)k−1 . (b) For every weak function semimeasure ν (every
order-independent predictor, Proposition 2.3), E[− log ν(Dk )] ≥ log(N − 1)k − log(N − 1) and
E[− log ν(f )] ≥ log((N − 1)!).
For k ≤ N/2 the two bounds differ by at most one bit. So no random-function law, and for
k ≤ N/2 no order-independent predictor, does better than SFI by more than O(K(k)) bits in
expectation.
Open question. Does the depth count, x,z ν(D(x, z)) ≤ N − k in the notation of Lemma E.2,
and hence (a), hold for every weak ν? A linear program confirms it for N = 4 only.
The normalised predictor is not order-free, and a set-based history-aware predictor has loss
0 here (Remark E.7), so information-theoretic arguments alone cannot bound it below. The
structure of MF can:

Proposition 6.7 (SFIhist ). [proved, given Corollary 5.18] examples Prop 2.5 (= history Prop 4.8) For every d,

Proposition 6.8 (SFIset ). [proved]
set
examples Prop 2.11 Let Lset be the loss of SFI , whose hypotheses see the set of previous inputs
(Proposition D.10). For uniform cyclic f on the full cycle, P[Lset < log((N − 1)!) − j − c] < 2−j ,
ELset ≥ log((N − 1)!), and always Lset ≤ log((N − 1)!) + c.

30

Proposition 6.9 (SI). [proved; computed (examples e6(f), an illustration)]
examples Prop 2.10 Let ρ ∈ {M, Mnorm } and f uniform cyclic. There is c, independent of d, with
ELyρ (k) ≤ c for every k ≤ N and P[∃k ≤ N : Lyρ (k) > j + c] ≤ 2−j .
SI pays about K(d) bits on the header 1d 0 of the first input, which tells it d; then the labels
are free. With raw d-bit blocks the bound becomes K(d) + c (Proposition E.4), probably the
right order (Conjecture E.5). So Hänni’s description holds: SFI pays about (d − log e)2d bits
over the cycle (semimeasure convention) and at least δd − O(log d) bits per step for t ≤ 2(1−δ)d
(normalised), while SI and SFIhist pay O(1), uniformly in d.
Remark E.7 locates the information and gives a separation in the other direction.

6.3

Eisenstat’s example

Definition 6.10 (Eisenstat’s example). examples §3.1; Hänni’s request Let b0 , b1 , . . . be i.i.d. Bern( 13 )
(index i is a head if bi = 1, else a tail) and ζ1 , ζ2 , . . . i.i.d. Bern( 32 ), independent of b. At step t,
reveal the smallest unrevealed head i as (i, 1) if ζt = 1, else the smallest unrevealed tail as (i, 0).
After n steps: Nn1 , Nn0 count the revealed heads and tails; τn (ηn ) is one more than the last revealed
tail (head), or 0; gn is the smallest unrevealed index; and U Bn := − log P1/3 (b<τn ) = τn log 3−Nn0 ,
with P1/3 the Bern( 13 ) product measure.
Hänni’s e-mail claims that SFI’s “log loss . . . is the loss given by our threshold function, up
to o(n) terms” (Hänni, 2026c). We take as threshold function the threshold law νsthr : the product
law with P[y = 1 | i] = 13 for i < s and = 1 for i ≥ s, a machine law with index computable from
s (Proposition 2.10(a)).
Lemma 6.11 (shape of the revealed set). [proved] examples Lemma 3.1 The revealed tails (heads) are
exactly the tails (heads) below τn (ηn ). No unrevealed index lies below min(τn , ηn ); an unrevealed
index below ηn is a tail, one below τn a head. If τn ≤ ηn , then gn ≥ τn and [τn , gn ) consists of
revealed heads.
Proof. The process reveals the first unrevealed head (tail), so the revealed heads (tails) are the
first Nn1 heads (Nn0 tails) of b.
So the revealed set is about [0, n/2) together with the heads in [n/2, 2n).
Proposition 6.12 (upper bound). [proved]
examples Prop 3.2 For every b, every reveal sequence of this kind (each choice of head or tail may be
arbitrary) and every n: LSFI (Dn ) ≤ U Bn + K(τn ) + c ≤ U Bn + log(τn + 1) + 2 log log(τn + 2) + c,
also for L̄SFI .
Indeed MF ≥ 2−K(τn )−c ντthr
(Theorem 2.9); under ντthr
the revealed indices at or above τn

are heads (Lemma 6.11) and cost nothing, and those below τn cost at most U Bn ; finally K(i) ≤
log(i + 1) + 2 log log(i + 2) + c.
Theorem 6.13 (pathwise sandwich). [proved] examples Thm 3.3 Semimeasure convention. For every
b, every reveal sequence and every n, LSFI (Dn ) ≥ K(b<gn | gn ) − c. If b is Martin-Löf random for
P1/3 , then at every n with τn ≤ ηn ,
U Bn − K(gn ) − cb ≤ LSFI (Dn ) ≤ U Bn + K(τn ) + c.
In Eisenstat’s process, almost surely |LSFI (Dn ) − U Bn | ≤ log n + O(log log n) + cb for all large n.

Remark 6.17 (Hänni’s e-mail argument). [proved; normalised open] examples §3.6 The claim (quoted in

History-aware prediction and SI. Call a history touching if its revealed set is the initial
segment [0, max(τ, η)). The Bayes-optimal history-aware predictor P ∗ is explicit (Lemma E.9):
at a touching history it gives the input max(τ, η) + k the label 1 with probability 2k /(2k + 1); at
every other history it is certain and correct.

A natural rule reads the labels off the history: the new input is a tail if and only if it is the

Corollary 6.20 (the rule). [proved] examples Cor 3.11 Almost surely there is a last touching step, and
the rule is exact at every later step.
Proposition 6.21 (errors of the rule). [proved; computed (examples e2, e7)] examples Prop 3.12 Over the
infinite run the rule errs twice in expectation, and it makes no error with probability exactly 12 .
In finite proxies (Table 4) order-free mixtures pay about 0.46 bits per step, history-aware and
joint ones a bounded total.
Open question. Is the normalised loss of the same order as LSFI over the whole cycle of
Definition 6.2, and in Eisenstat’s example? The method of Proposition 6.6 pays O(log t) bits per
step, more than the information per step near the end of the cycle and in Eisenstat’s example
(0.46 bits), and set-based history-aware predictors have loss O(1) in both. In finite mixtures with
most prior on partial chains the full-cycle normalised loss is far below log((N − 1)!) (19.2 against
40.25 bits at d = 4).

Proposition 6.27 (frozen label noise). [proved; computed (examples e4(c))] examples Prop 4.6 Let g :
