The machine behind (a) shares a flip rate across inputs, so it is a mixture of product laws
(Appendix E.4). Fresh label noise is different: random-function SFI fails as soon as an input can
repeat with another label (Remark E.10).

7

Out-of-distribution behaviour

Hänni’s notes state that if “there is some simple property such that no input with that property
showed up in training”, SFI keeps “at least corresponding const probability of just outputting
0 on all inputs with that property”, and ask whether “some log term” is lost (Hänni, 2026b).
His draft adds a two-sided version, asks for a converse, and lists examples where SFI “plausibly”
generalises well. This section makes these statements precise. His notes use plain complexity
C(·); we use prefix complexity K throughout.
Conventions. ν ranges over {W, MF } (Definitions 2.15 and 3.1); for W the normalised conditional ν̄ is defined as M̄F is for MF . For a target f and inputs x1 , x2 , . . . , Dk := {(xj , f (xj )) :
j ≤ k} and the posterior error ek := 1 − ν(f (xk ) | Dk−1 , xk ) is the posterior mass that is wrong
or undefined at xk . At a test point x∗ with training labels f |A : e(x∗ ) := 1 − ν(f (x∗ ) | f |A , x∗ );
P
ewr (x∗ ) := y̸=f (x∗ ) ν(f |A + (x∗ , y))/ν(f |A ) (defined and wrong); enorm (x∗ ) := 1 − ν̄(f (x∗ ) |
P
f |A , x∗ ). Since y ν(f |A + (x∗ , y)) ≤ ν(f |A ),
ewr (x∗ ) ≤ enorm (x∗ ) ≤ e(x∗ ),

(3)

so every lower bound below on ewr , and every upper bound on e, holds in both conventions. A
property Q ⊆ X separates x∗ from A if x∗ ∈ Q and Q ∩ A = ∅. For decidable Q and partial
computable b, K(Q, b) is the complexity of a pair ⟨e, e′ ⟩ with φe a total decider of Q and φe′
computing b on Q ∩ dom b; K(Q, b) ≤ K(Q) + K(b) + c.

7.1

Unseen simple properties

Proposition 7.1 (Hänni’s proposition, with no log term). [proved]
context Prop 2.1 Let Q be decidable, b partial computable, dom D ∩ Q = ∅, S ⊆ Q ∩ dom b finite and
E := b|S . Then (a) ν(D ∪ E) ≥ 2−K(Q,b)−c ν(D), and the hypotheses (programs for W , tapes for
MF ) consistent with D that agree with b on all of Q ∩ dom b have mass at least 2−K(Q,b)−c ν(D);
(b) KF (D ∪ E) ≤ KF (D) + K(Q, b) + c.
Proof. For W : let V ′ read a V -program z for ⟨e, e′ ⟩, then a V -program p, and output an index
of the function equal to φe′ on Q and to φV (p) off Q; let V (wu) = V ′ (u). The map p 7→ wzp
is injective, preserves consistency with D (no input of D lies in Q), and its image agrees with b
on Q ∩ dom b. Summing over consistent p with |z| = K(Q, b) gives (a); a shortest p gives (b).
For MF : the function machine TQ,b that answers b(x) on Q without reading the tape and runs
TU off Q has an index computable from ⟨e, e′ ⟩, and maps every tape with fω ⊇ D to a function
extending D ∪ b|Q∩dom b ; apply Theorem 2.9.
34

So, whatever D is and however large, SFI behaves like b on all unseen Q-inputs with posterior
probability at least 2−K(Q,b)−c ; with b ≡ 0 this is Hänni’s statement. For |S| = 1 and b wrong
there, (a) bounds ewr , so it holds in both conventions.
Remark 7.2 (where a log term would come from). [proved] context Rem 2.2 No log term arises, for W
or for MF : the modified program is a concatenation of self-delimiting codes, and the proof sums
over all consistent programs. Plain complexity costs K(Q) ≤ C(Q) + 2 log C(Q) + O(1). If Q is
only semi-decidable the argument gives no bound at all, since the wrapper cannot decide Q; a
time-truncated decidable subset of Q restores it there.
Hänni also worries about properties that are simple only relative to a good training program
(“if this internal variable is set 0, then the if statement triggers”). Conditional complexity captures
this.
Proposition 7.3 (properties simple given a training-consistent program). [proved (corrected)]
context Prop 2.3 Let p ∈ dom V be consistent with D, and q, r indices with φq (⟨p, ·⟩) total; let Qp :=
{x : φq (⟨p, x⟩) = 1} and bp (x) := φr (⟨p, x⟩). If Qp ∩ dom D = ∅ and S ⊆ Qp ∩ dom bp is finite,
then ν(D ∪ bp |S ) ≥ 2−K(q,r|p)−c 2−|p| ; so the posterior probability of bp on S is at least 2−K(q,r|p)−c
times 2−|p| /ν(D), the posterior weight of p.
Proof idea. The program wpz reads p first, then a conditional program z for ⟨q, r⟩ given p; it
outputs bp on Qp and p’s values elsewhere. Reading z first would give the complexity of the pair
instead. For MF use MF ≥ 2−c W (Theorem 3.4).
Proposition 7.4 (dependence on the universal machine). [proved; analogue known] context Prop 2.4
Fix V , a decidable Q and a partial computable b. (a) Some universal prefix machine V ′ has
2−K(Q,b)−c W ′ ≤ W ≤ 2W ′ and, for every D with dom D ∩ Q = ∅ and finite S ⊆ Q ∩ dom b,
W ′ (D) = W (D) and W ′ (D ∪ b|S ) ≥ 21 W ′ (D). (b) Some universal prefix machine V ′′ yields an
SFI MF′ with 2−K(Q,b)−c MF′ ≤ MF ≤ 2MF′ on nonempty data sets such that, for every nonempty
D with dom D ∩ Q = ∅, MF′ (D) = MF (D) and the MF′ -posterior probability that fω = b on all of
Q ∩ dom b is at least 12 .
Proof idea (Appendix F). V ′ runs V on programs 0u and the wrapper of Proposition 7.1 on
programs 1u; the halves agree off Q. So Hänni’s remark that “there’s a programming language
in which [good behaviour] doesn’t have a better const” is right. The machine is tailored to one
property and behaviour, K(Q, b) + O(1) bits from the reference machine. This is a supervised
analogue of the dependence of AIXI on the reference machine (Leike and Hutter, 2015a).

7.2

The draft’s two-sided result

For a property P ⊆ X × Y of input–output pairs (“y is a correct proof of x”), Hänni’s draft states
that “the probability of P -abiding generalization is at least 2−C(P ) ” and, if no training input
has property Q, “at most 2−C(Q) times 2 to the negative complexity of the smallest string not
satisfying P on the given input times a const”; in sum, good OOD generalisation “has at least a
const probability of happening but also at least some const probability of not happening” (Hänni,
2026b). The summary is correct:
Theorem 7.5 (two-sided bound on P -abiding generalisation). [proved] context Thm 2.5 Let Q be
decidable, dom D ∩ Q = ∅, S ⊆ Q finite and nonempty, and K(P, Q) the complexity of a decider
of Q paired with an enumerator of P (in (a)) or of its complement (in (b)). (a) If P is c.e. and
every x ∈ Q has some y with (x, y) ∈ P , then ν(every x ∈ S gets a P -abiding output | D) ≥

/ P , then

ν(some x ∈ S gets a P -violating output | D) ≥ 2

/ P : MF (D + (x, z)) ≥ 2−K(Q)−K(z|x)−c MF (D) and
P
W (D + (x, z)) ≥ 2−K(Q)−K (z|x)−c W (D) (Remark B.1).
Proof idea (Appendix F). (a), (b) apply Proposition 7.1 to the first abiding (violating) output
found by enumeration; (c) runs a conditional program on half of the tape on Q and TU on the
other half elsewhere. The bounds hold for every amount of data. The draft’s upper bound must
be read as a lower bound on the probability of violation, as in (c). Literally, both bounds are
false:
Proposition 7.6 (the draft’s bounds as stated are false). [refuted; proved]
context Prop 2.6 Let P = {(x, 0) : x ∈ X}. (a) If D labels inputs outside a decidable Qd with 0 and
x∗ ∈ Qd , then ν((x∗ , 0) | D) ≥ 2−c , while the literal upper bound tends to 0 as K(Qd ) → ∞. (b)
If x∗ ∈ {0, 1}d has K(x∗ | d) ≥ d and D = {(x, 1) : x ∈ {0, 1}d \ {x∗ }}, then K(P ) = O(1) but
ν((x∗ , 0) | D) ≤ 2−d+c , also normalised. With Q = {x∗ } Theorem 7.5(a) gives 2−d−2 log d−c , so its
K(Q) term is needed and nearly tight.

Remark 7.9 (the novelty bound is lopsided). [refuted as stated; remark]

if a computable x-only predictor generalises correctly across all the shifts, SFI’s total excess log
loss over it is at most its complexity plus c. A human who generalises after seeing the data is
history-aware, which needs Corollary 5.18; reading Proposition 7.8 as a formalisation of Hänni’s
“fake ood generalization” is an interpretation, not a theorem.

Proof idea (Appendix F). The hypotheses that agree with f on B \ {x} and are defined and

So if f is simple and no property simple given the labelled sample separates the test point, the

the difference is the value of knowing the other inputs. The mass undefined at x∗ is not lsc, so
there is no pointwise bound on e(x∗ ); Corollary 4.14 bounds it in distribution.
Example 7.13 (easy-to-hard generalisation; shortest proofs). [proved] context Ex 2.15, 2.15a Let the
inputs be sentences of a consistent theory T with decidable proof checking, labelled 1 if T proves
them and 0 if it refutes them. Let every training sentence have a proof or refutation of length
at most L, and the test sentence x∗ none of length at most L′ := 2⌈log L⌉ ≤ 2L. The property
“no proof or refutation of length ≤ L′ ” is decidable, separates x∗ from the training set, and has
37

complexity at most K(T ) + K(⌈log L⌉) + c ≤ K(T ) + 2 log log L + c for large L. With b ≡ 0 and
b ≡ 1, Proposition 7.1 gives the wrong label probability at least 2−K(T )−2 log log L−c , however many
easy sentences were seen. Proof search, of length K(T ) + c, fits every correctly labelled training
set, so the correct label has probability at least 2−K(T )−c . If instead each provable sentence is
labelled by its shortest proof, Theorem 7.5(c) with the violating output ε (K(ε | x∗ ) = O(1))
gives probability at least 2−K(T )−2 log log L−c that x∗ does not get its shortest proof.
Both ends are independent of the amount of data. The failure floor is not independent of the
difficulty threshold: it decays, slowly, as L grows.
Remark 7.14 (the strongest true positive statements). [remark] context §2.5 (i) Over any shifts, SFI’s
total loss exceeds that of every computable x-only predictor P by at most K(P ) + c, and its
total error is at most (ln 2)(K(f ) + c) (Theorems 4.1 and 7.7). (ii) A random train/test split of
any input set B costs at most Lν (f |B )/|B| in expectation (Proposition 4.10). (iii) For simple f
∗
∗
the error at a test point lies between 2− sep(x ;A)−O(1) and 2Lν (f )−sep(x ;A|f )+O(1) ; for random f
no learner has a converse. So “no simple separating property implies small error” fails only for
bounds independent of K(f ), or for properties simple unconditionally. (iv) In distribution the
per-step error is at most min{((K(f ) + c) ln 2 + ln n + 1)/n, 2K(f )+c /(n + 1)}, and for uniform
inputs at least of order 2−K(f ) /(n log2 n) (Proposition 4.13 and Corollary 4.14).
Open question. Is there a positive statement for properties simple given a program fitted to
the training data only (as in Proposition 7.10(c)), with a factor depending on K(f )?

7.5

Hänni’s examples

Hänni’s examples are sentences of a formal theory labelled by provability (“assuming the riemann
hypothesis is decidable, it seems plausible that solomonoff function induction gives it the intended
label”), sentences labelled by their shortest proofs, and “correctly counted the lengths of odd
strings”, tested on even lengths (Hänni, 2026b).
Proposition 7.15 (the intended answer). [proved] examples Prop 5.1 If the labels follow a partial
computable g and x ∈ dom g is new, SFI gives g(x) predictive probability at least 2LSFI (D)−K(g)−c ,
in both conventions.
Proof. MF (D + (x, g(x))) ≥ 2−K(g)−c (Theorem 4.1(b)); divide by MF (D); the normalised conditional is larger.
This is near 1 only if LSFI (D) ≈ K(g), i.e. if g-like programs carry most of the posterior.
Example 7.16 (Hänni’s three examples). [proved; computed (examples e5)]
examples §5.1–5.3; context Ex 2.15, 2.15a Let T be a consistent theory with decidable proof checking, and
pT its proof search, of length K(T ) + c. (a) Provability labels. pT fits every correctly labelled
training set, so LSFI (D) ≤ K(T ) + c, Proposition 7.15 applies, and over any sequence of sentences
the correct label gets probability at most 12 at most K(T ) + c times. A hard test sentence
keeps a wrong-label probability of at least 2−K(T )−2 log log L−c however many easy sentences were
seen (Example 7.13). This makes precise Hänni’s weakly held hypothesis that for Solomonoff
induction “ood generalization is a thing, but easy-to-hard generalization is not”. Relative to
proof search in the form “fixed searcher, then a description of T ” (Proposition 7.3), the floor is
′
′
2−K(L |pT )−c ≥ 2−c /(log L(log log L)2 ) times the posterior weight of pT . (b) Shortest proofs as
labels. The same bounds hold, with the floor of Example 7.13. (c) Lengths of odd strings. The
38

correct answer has probability at least 2−c on every even-length input and SFI makes at most c
online errors; “|x| even” and “output 0” cost O(1) bits, so a wrong answer keeps probability at least
′
2−c . Both constants depend on the machine. An exact computation over a small enumerated
grammar of programs (Appendix F; code in the accompanying repository) gives the intended
answer probability at least 0.979, 0.997 or 0.9997 on every even-length test string, depending on
one prior weight, and about 0.11 when one extra program tilts the grammar.
Table 6 (Appendix F) summarises what is proved, what is only plausible, and natural strengthenings of Hänni’s statements that are false.
Conjecture 7.17 (natural machines generalise as intended). [conjecture] examples §5.1–5.3, §7 item 4
For natural universal machines and natural training sets, the intended answers of Example 7.16
get posterior probability close to 1; for example, a decidable Riemann hypothesis gets its correct
label with high probability.
This is not a precise statement, and by Proposition 7.4 it fails for some universal machines;
what is machine-invariant is the bounded number of errors (Theorem 7.7). We know no route to
a version for a fixed reference machine.

8

Computable versions

SFI is not computable (Proposition 2.24). Hänni’s notes sketch two computable relatives: a timebounded Solomonoff predictor with constant regret against all predictors of a given running time,
and induction over Boolean circuits, where “regret and complexity are polynomially related”
(Hänni, 2026b). This section states what holds and shows that Occam (simplest-consistent)
learning, unlike Bayes, fails exponentially out of distribution.

8.1

Time-bounded SFI

Definition 8.1 (time-bounded predictors; length-activated mixture). context §3.1 Let Y be finite,
|Y | ≥ 2. A probability predictor is a RAM program A that on input x halts with rationals
(A(y | x))y∈Y , nonnegative and summing to 1, in time TA (x). For a nondecreasing t : N → N≥1 ,

Remark 8.3 (distinct inputs are a real restriction). [refuted; computed (context c9)] context §3.1 One

hypotheses are mixed round by round, length clocking leaves a comparator over budget on short
inputs forever. On 4000 rounds that repeat 12 short inputs with labels drawn from a comparator
in Ct , a direct computation gives regret 469 bits, growing linearly.

Remark 8.6 (Hänni’s “apples to oranges” caveat). [remark] context Rem 3.2 In Hänni’s sequence theorem the learner reuses state across rounds while the comparator recomputes, which he calls
comparing “apples to oranges”. For x-only comparators, which see one input and have nothing to
reuse, Theorem 8.2 and Proposition 8.4 compare like with like. History-aware comparators need
a chronological learner: no x-only learner competes with them, since the chain and skipped-string
predictors run in linear time while SFI’s loss on those data is unbounded (Theorems 5.3 and 5.4).

Proposition 8.7 (time-bounded history-aware predictors). [regret proved; running time proof sketch]
context Prop 3.2c Let Cthist be the RAM programs A that keep a state, receive xk , output A(· | hk−1 , xk ),
then receive yk , with round k taking at most cA t(ñk ) steps, where ñk is the length of the encoded
hk−1 and xk . Let the learner start machine i at round ki and give it 2g(k)t(ñk ) steps in round k,
spent replaying the stored history and then on round k; it uses Ai ’s output if this finishes in time
and u otherwise, and mixes the per-round products. Then the regret against every A = Ai ∈ Cthist
is at most log(1/wi ) + 2k0 log |Y | on every stream, k0 := max(ki , min{k : g(k) ≥ cA }); round k
costs O(#{i : ki ≤ k} g(k)t(ñk )) steps plus the replay memory.
Proof idea (Appendix G). As ñk grows, every past round of A fits into the current budget
once k ≥ k0 , so the replay catches up by round 2k0 − 2 and stays caught up. This is the functionsetting form of Hänni’s amortised warm-up; a budget depending only on |xk | could stall. The class
contains the chain and skipped-string predictors; it is the time-bounded counterpart of SFIhist
(Corollary 5.18).

(Schmidhuber, 2002; Filan et al., 2016) treat the sequence case.

8.2

Circuit SFI

Definition 8.9 (circuits and the circuit mixture). context §3.2 Fix d ≥ 1 and the basis of all 16 twoinput Boolean gates. A circuit of size s is a list of s gates, gate j with a type and two inputs from
Q
{x1 , . . . , xd , 0, 1, gate1 , . . . , gatej−1 }; gate s is the output. There are Ns = sj=1 16(d + 1 + j)2
of them. The prior w(C) := 2−s /Ns is the code length of “size in unary, then the gate list”;
P
P
{w(C) : C consistent with D}, size(f ) the least size of a
C w(C) = 1. Let Wcirc (D) :=
d
circuit for f , and for B ⊆ {0, 1} , sizeB (f ) the least size of one agreeing with f on B, and
LB := − log Wcirc (f |B ). The exception gadget appends γ ≤ d + 1 gates computing [x = a] and a
new output gate, so that C ′ equals C off a and outputs a chosen bit at a; C 7→ C ′ is injective and
w(C ′ )/w(C) ≥ 2−Γ(s) , Γ(s) := γ(5 + 2 log(d + 1 + s + γ)).
For any ordering of B, let Pi be the posterior probability of the wrong label at step i (circuits
P
are total, so both conventions agree) and E := i Pi the expected number of mistakes of posterior
sampling (Gibbs mistakes).

41

Theorem 8.10 (circuit Bayes: mistakes and circuit size). [proved; computed (context c1, c8)] context
Thm 3.5 For every B ⊆ {0, 1}d , every f and every ordering of B: (a) some circuit of size at most
⌈LB ⌉ agrees with f on B, and LB ≤ sizeB (f ) + log NsizeB (f ) = O(sizeB (f ) log(sizeB (f ) + d)); (b)
E ≤ (ln 2)LB and LB ≤ 2E(Γ(⌈LB ⌉) + 3); explicitly, for all E > 0 and d ≥ 1, sizeB (f ) ≤ ⌈LB ⌉
with LB ≤ 16 E (d + 1) log 2(E + 1)(d + 2) .
Proof idea (Appendix G). (a) Circuits larger than S have total weight 2−S . (b) The first
inequality is Pi ≤ − ln(1 − Pi ) with Proposition 2.16. For the second, the gadget maps the small
wrong circuits at step i injectively into the right ones at weight cost 2−Γ , so − log(1 − Pi ) ≤ Γ + 2;
steps with Pi ≤ 21 cost at most 2Pi , and at most 2E steps have Pi > 21 . A grid computation gives
the largest ratio 10.85 in place of 16.
Remark 8.11 (relation to Hänni’s sketch). [remark; computed (context c1)]
context Rem 3.6, 3.7 Hänni’s direction, “expected regret m when learning the function f implies there
is a circuit of size O(m log m · inputdim)”, is (b) with (a), with log((E + 1)(d + 2)) for log m; it
holds for every single ordering, needs no Markov step (total log loss is order independent), and
his flagged “+O(log m) term” is the log(d + s) in Γ. His step from “log loss ≤ poly(m)” to “a
circuit of size poly(m)” is a circuit coding theorem; (a) gives it for a size prior with exponential
tail, while a polynomial tail gives only size(f ) ≤ 2O(L) . The converse holds on every ordering:
E ≤ (ln 2)L ≤ (ln 2) size(f )(5 + 2 log(d + 1 + size(f ))). For formulas on d = 3 an exhaustive
computation over all 256 functions and all orderings confirms inequalities of the same form.
Proposition 8.12 (Occam learning in distribution). [(a) known; (b) proved] context Props 3.8, 3.9 Order
functions by shortest description length in a prefix-free language, then by a fixed tie rule; ℓ(f ) is
the shortest description length of f . The Occam learner predicts with the first function consistent
with the data, so it uses only Hf := {h preceding or equal to f }, |Hf | < 2ℓ(f )+1 . For i.i.d. inputs
from µ: (a) after n ≥ (ln |Hf | + ln(1/δ))/ε samples its hypothesis has µ-error at most ε with
probability at least 1 − δ (Blumer et al., 1987); (b) its expected number of mistakes is at most
6.7 + (ln |Hf |)(1 + ln n0 ) + ln2 n0 , n0 := ⌈ln |Hf |/pmin ⌉ with pmin the least positive point mass of
µ; for uniform µ on {0, 1}d this is O(ℓ(f )(d + log ℓ(f )) + d2 ).
(a) is a union bound over Hf ; with ε = δ = 0.01 it needs 70ℓ(f ) + 531 samples, so Hänni’s
“1000000 · C(f ) input-output pairs” suffice when C(f ) is description length. (b) is proved in
Appendix G.
Remark 8.13 (the “physicist’s proof” is a proof for weighted majority). [proved; known] context Rem

3.10 Hänni’s “physicist’s proof” that Occam makes O(C(f )) online mistakes on every ordering

assumes that when the current guess is wrong, a constant fraction of the competing weight is
wrong too. That is the guarantee of the posterior-majority (weighted-majority) predictor: when
it errs, at least half of the posterior mass errs, so Wcirc (Di ) ≤ Wcirc (Di−1 )/2, and it makes at
most LB ≤ ℓ(f ) mistakes on every ordering (Littlestone, 1988; Littlestone and Warmuth, 1994).
Under the circuit prior the maximum-a-posteriori circuit is a smallest consistent one, so the
MAP-hypothesis learner is an Occam learner. For Occam the assumption can fail at every step:

8.3

Occam learning fails out of distribution

The construction below is from the Vaintrob–Hänni slides (Appendix K) (Vaintrob and Hänni,
2026b); we prove it for two languages and every tie rule. Formulas have leaves xj , ¬xj , 0, 1 and
binary nodes AND, OR, XOR; size is the number of leaves; the prior is “size in unary, then
uniform within the size”. Circuits are as in Definition 8.9.
42

Theorem 8.14 (the conjunction ladder). [proved; computed (context c6, c11); construction from the
slides] context Thm 3.11 Let f = x1 ∧ · · · ∧ xd , and let Occam use any fixed tie rule among functions
of equal least size, for formulas (d ≥ 2) or circuits (d ≥ 3). Present 1d , then 0d , then the
inputs of Hamming weight 1, 2, . . . , d − 1 level by level, inside a level an input on which the
current hypothesis errs if there is one. Then Occam makes at least 2d − 2 mistakes (formulas) or
2d − d − 2 (circuits). Since ℓ(f ) = Θ(d log d) in both languages, its worst-case number of mistakes
is 2Ω(ℓ/ log ℓ) ; the trivial upper bound is 2ℓ+1 .
Proof idea (Appendix G). At the start of stage k the data say h(1d ) = 1 and h(x) = 0 for
|x| < k. A Möbius-inversion
lemma (Lemma G.2) shows that the least-size consistent functions
d
are then exactly the k conjunctions of k variables (for circuits from k = 2). Each is wrong only
at its own point 1S , so every input of weight k is a mistake. The ordering is computable from d
and the tie rule, so “structured orderings are safe” is false, as the slides note. A second adversary
gives the circuit count without the lemma (Remark G.3). A direct computation (d = 3, 4, 5, seven
tie rules) finds every input of every covered level to be a mistake.
Remark 8.15 (it is the selection rule, not the prior). [computed (context c6(e)); known; unverified
(claimed in the slides)] context Rem 3.12 With the same formula prior the posterior-majority predictor
makes at most L(f ) ≤ ℓ(f ) mistakes on every ordering (Remark 8.13). An exact computation
(code in the accompanying repository; ties count 12 ) gives, for d = 3 and d = 4: Occam on the
ladder 7 and 15 mistakes; posterior majority on the ladder 2.5 and 3.5, and on its worst ordering
5.5 and 8.5; Gibbs on its worst ordering 4.47 and 7.08; halving bound L(f ) = 10.83 and 15.30.
The slides tabulate four selection rules on one prior: deterministic argmax, 2Θ(C/ log C) worst-case
mistakes; argmax with randomised tie-breaking, Θ̃(C 2 ); a uniformly random consistent hypothesis
with a doubling budget, O(C); weighted majority, at most C. They also state that a random
ordering gives O((C +d)d) expected mistakes in any language, and the descending-weight ordering
exactly d + 1. The first and last rows are proved here (Theorem 8.14 and Remark 8.13); the other
claims are the slides’ and we have not checked them.
Remark 8.16 (uniform tie-breaking on the ladder). [proved] context Rem 3.13 All formulas of one size
have the same prior weight, so the limit λ → ∞ of the power posterior ∝ w(formula)λ 1[consistent]
(the direction of the companion paper on steeper priors, Hänni 2026a) is uniform over the leastsize consistent formulas, and by symmetry uniform over the surviving k-conjunctions at stage k.
P
2
On the ladder it makes at most 1 + d−1
k=1 H(d) ≤ d + d ln 2 Gibbs mistakes (Hm the harmonic
k

number) and at most 32 (d − 1) + 1 majority mistakes. So Theorem 8.14 is a failure of deterministic
tie-breaking; it does not show that steeper SFI fails on this ordering.

Open questions. A circuit coding theorem for size priors with polynomial tails: do large random circuits rarely compute functions without small circuits? Do few posterior-majority mistakes
imply a small circuit (steps with Pi just below 12 cost log loss but no majority mistakes)? Is there
a natural language and target with 2Ω(ℓ) worst-case Occam mistakes, and can a steeper posterior
over a universal machine fail exponentially on some ordering? The machine-model details of the
running times above (RAM versus multitape, the cost of the clocks) are not written out.

9

Neural-network Bayes and circuit SFI

Hänni describes Bayesian learning with neural networks (NN Bayes) as “kinda like a circuit
version of solomonoff function induction” (Hänni, 2026c), referring to slides by Vaintrob and
43

Hänni (Vaintrob and Hänni, 2026b). This section states what is proved about this link, what the
slides claim, and what is open. An architecture is a parameter space Θ with a prior ϖ and a map
θ 7→ fθ : {0, 1}d → Y .
Proposition 9.1 (NN Bayes is function-space Bayes). [proved (trivial)] context Prop 4.1 If the likelihood
depends on θ only through the values of fθ on the data (a 0–1 likelihood, a margin rule, any labelnoise model), the network’s posterior predictive equals the function-space Bayes predictive with
prior PA (f ) := ϖ{θ : fθ = f }.
Proof. Group the parameter integral by the value of fθ .
So NN Bayes is function induction with the prior PA , and the question is how PA compares
with 2−K , with MF , or with a circuit prior.
Proposition 9.2 (coding-theorem upper bound). [proved (given the coding theorem)] context Prop 4.2
If PA , as a semimeasure on truth tables, is lower-semicomputed by a program a (encoding the
′
architecture, the prior and d), then PA (f ) ≤ 2−K(f |a)+c ≤ 2−K(f )+K(a)+c , with c independent of
the architecture.
This is the conditional coding theorem for the family indexed by a. PA is lsc for finite
parameter sets with rational probabilities; for Gaussian priors on real weights with sign outputs
we only sketch it (up to a null boundary, {θ : fθ = f } is an open semialgebraic set, approximable
from below by certified rational boxes). Hänni’s version has an extra log log(1/p) term (Hänni,
2026b), which is not needed. The bound is an exact, uncomputable form of the simplicity-bias
bound of Dingle et al. (2018).
Proposition 9.3 (every prior nearly saturates the bound on its typical set). [proved; known (standard)] context Prop 4.3 For every semimeasure P on truth tables, every string a and j ≥ 0: P {f :
P (f ) ≤ 2−K(f |a)−j } ≤ 2−j . Hence if PA is lower-semicomputed by a and ϖ is a probability measure, then with probability at least 1−2−j over θ ∼ ϖ, K(fθ | a)−c ≤ − log PA (fθ ) < K(fθ | a)+j.
Proof. The left side is at most f 2−K(f |a)−j ≤ 2−j by Kraft’s inequality; the lower bound is
Proposition 9.2.
This is the standard fact that − log P ≈ K on P -most objects (Li and Vitányi, 2008) (cf.
Lemma 6.1). It confirms Hänni’s remark that the result “should just be true about any low
complexity prior on functions — the NNs aren’t doing anything here”: PA looks like 2−K on its
own typical set, which can omit most simple functions.
P

The slides make the point of (a) with functions that diagonalise against all small circuits. So
the right comparison object for networks is circuit SFI, not program SFI. For the uniform prior
over circuits of one size, one half of the comparison holds (Definition 8.9 for Ns and size):
Proposition 9.5 (uniform circuit priors contain the circuit simplicity prior). [proved] context Prop
4.5 Let Punif,S be uniform over circuits of size exactly S. For every f with size(f ) < S, Punif,S (f ) ≥
1/(Nsize(f ) ·16(d+1+S)2 ). Hence Bayes with this prior makes at most (ln 2)(log Nsize(f ) +2 log(d+
1 + S) + 4) = O(size(f ) log(size(f ) + d) + log S) Gibbs mistakes on every ordering.
44

Proof idea (Appendix H). Pad a minimal circuit with arbitrary dead gates and a final gate
copying its output. This is one half of Hänni’s “first main afaik-open problem” (Hänni, 2026b);
the other half, that the uniform prior is not much larger than the simplicity prior on functions
without small circuits, is open.
Remark 9.6 (what a bridge would give). [remark] context §4.3 If PA (f ) ≥ 2−aℓ(f )−b for a description

which Hänni thought “true for NN bayes as well”, is claimed by the slides in their setting and in

The Gaussian-process limits. Hänni suggests that in the infinite-width Gaussian limits
(NNGP and NTK) the network is “doing sth more like a polyfit” than circuit SFI (Hänni, 2026b).

Proof idea (Appendix H). (a) K(x, x′ ) depends only on x ⊙ x′ , so characters are eigenfunctions;

Remark 9.10 (the Vaintrob–Hänni slides). [known; unverified (claimed in the slides); warm-up arithmetic
re-checked (context c8)] context §4.5; slides 10–17, 29–37, App. A, F, J, K The slides warn that their appendices
“have largely been generated by Claude” and have not been “meticulously reviewed”, that the
main results summarise “much more caveated and detailed versions”, that only the strong-prior
case is proved in the talk, and that Bayesian learning and SGD are “importantly different”. They
claim: (i) for programs with prior ∝ 3−|p| and i.i.d. inputs, O(C) samples (C the length of the
shortest program for f ) give average posterior probability above 99.9% on the correct answer, with
probability 1 − exp(−Ω(C)), and a “relevance-based” sample-complexity bound for any countable
class; (ii) for an MLP of depth L and any width N ≥ C := CL (f ) (the least width and weight
bound of a depth-L MLP implementing f ), with bounded, Lipschitz, non-polynomial activation
and Gaussian weights in the strong-prior limit, poly(d, C) samples suffice in the same sense, the
polynomial hiding a factor exponential in depth; (iii) the orderings of functions by CL , by a
layer-weighted squared norm κN and by prior mass are “scrambled only polynomially”, uniformly
in width; (iv) an in-distribution mistake bound poly(d, CL (f )) in expectation, and “the circuit
complexity CL of the learned function is at most poly(d, #mistakes made while learning it)”; (v) a
mean-field analogue (Vaintrob and Hänni, 2026a), while the NNGP “does not have a good circuit
prior”; (vi) out of distribution the guarantee “is simply false” (Theorem 8.14). We re-checked
the proof and arithmetic of (i), the character argument of their Appendix J (Theorem 9.7(a))
and the ladder of Appendix K; the rest is unchecked. Corollary 4.14(a) is the Markov half of
the relevance-based bound, applied to W and MF . Claim (iii) concerns orderings, not two-sided
bounds on prior mass; the slides list “a lower bound on the log prior of a function” as open.
Remark 9.11 (what can be said rigorously). [remark] context §4.6 Proved: NN Bayes is function-space
Bayes; its prior is at most 2−K+O(K(a)) , nearly equal on its typical set, with no general lower bound,
so the comparison object is circuit SFI; uniform circuit priors contain the circuit simplicity prior;
the Gaussian-process limits fail on sparse and odd parities where SFI succeeds; and for circuit
Bayes, mistakes and circuit size are polynomially related on every ordering (Theorem 8.10), while
Occam selection on the same prior fails exponentially out of distribution (Theorem 8.14). Claimed
in the slides: the strong-prior theorems. Open: two-sided bounds on PA (f ) at fixed σ, and whether
NN Bayes generalises like SGD-trained networks at all, which Hänni doubts (Hänni, 2026b).
Remark 9.12 (simplicity-bias literature). [known (snippets; unverified)] context §4.7 Valle-Pérez et al.
(2019) argue, following Dingle et al. (2018), that the parameter–function map of deep networks is
exponentially biased towards simple functions. Hänni’s objection (Hänni, 2026b) that the upper
bound holds for every map while the needed lower bound fails is made rigorous by Propositions 9.3
and 9.4; the slides argue the same. Mingard et al. (2021) report that SGD samples functions
with probabilities close to the Bayesian posterior, and Mingard et al. (2025) attribute much of
generalisation to a prior bias towards simple functions; we take no position. Buzaglo et al. (2024)
46

show that random interpolators under a flat parameter prior generalise when a narrow teacher
exists; the slides call this the closest prior work and note that its bound degrades with student
width. Goldblum et al. (2024) relate no-free-lunch theorems to Kolmogorov complexity, and
Grau-Moya et al. (2024) meta-train networks on data from universal machines to approximate
Solomonoff induction. These descriptions follow the abstracts.
Open questions. The upper half of the uniform-circuit bridge (Proposition 9.5); a lower bound
on the NN log prior at fixed σ; a check of the slides’ main theorem and of “mistakes imply a small
circuit”; the per-point test error of GP classification on odd parities; a proof that kernel-ridge
sign accuracy on unseen points falls below 21 .

10

Steeper simplicity priors (summary of the companion paper)

Hänni’s notes propose to penalise “the length of the program plus the negative log likelihood . . .
but with a higher coefficient on the length of the program”, in function induction where “each
input gets its own private randomness”, so that the selected hypothesis is a simple model plus
noise rather than a model plus memorised errors of the teacher (Hänni, 2026b; Hänni and Wyeth,
undated). The companion paper (Hänni, 2026a) studies this proposal. It works with classes of
hypotheses indexed by programs π of length ℓ(π), the objective λℓ(π) − log Pπ (D) with λ ≥ 1, and
the power posterior ∝ 2−λℓ(π) Pπ (D). With λ proportional to the sample size n these are batch
objectives at each n, not one Bayesian predictor. This section summarises the results; all of them
are proved there.
The absorption objection. A standard point, which Hänni’s note already makes for sequence
prediction, is that under a steeper penalty a hypothesis that is itself a universal mixture absorbs
everything. The companion paper’s absorption theorem makes this exact: in any class that
contains its own mixture U as a member of length ℓU (SI; SFI with shared randomness, U = MF ;
the weak class, U = M w ; SFIhist , U = MH , which is universal by Corollary 5.18), every λ-MAP has
length at most (λℓU +O(1))/(λ−1) on every data set and fits almost as well as U , so a deterministic
teacher’s errors are reproduced. In the class of product hypotheses (private randomness per input,
Hänni’s x-only samplers) no member is universal: every fixed product hypothesis loses 2d − 1 bits
on some parity on {0, 1}d that has a program of length d + 2 log d + O(1), and the universal
conditional semimeasure never learns. So steeper SFI is defined over the product class, whose
λ → 1 limit is Mprod , not MF . This restriction has a price. At λ = 1 it is the gap between
Mprod and MF , unbounded but at most K(dom D) + O(1) bits (Proposition 3.7 and Remark 3.8).
At λ > 1, on labels that share a latent fair coin between pairs of inputs, the product class pays
min(λ, 2) bits per coin against 1 bit for MF . The protection ends where hypotheses see earlier
labelled pairs: the in-context Bayes mixture is then one product hypothesis again. The paper
presents the restriction to private randomness (per input; on data sets the same as per query,
Proposition 4.7) as a modelling choice: the model is the part of the description reused across
inputs.
The teacher’s mistakes. Hänni’s example has a 100-bit simple model and systematic errors on
a 2−10 fraction of the inputs whose set takes 10,000 bits. The companion paper realises it rigorously
with a random parity on {0, 1}100 and an error rule on the first 20 input bits (11,710 bits),
quantifying over all product hypotheses by list decoding. For error rules that are incompressible
47

relative to the model, the teacher’s-mistakes theorem gives a window of λ/n from 2−20 ≈ 9.54·10−7
up to 8.06 · 10−3 (machine constant set to 0, an illustration) or 3.66 · 10−3 (machine constant 30).
Inside it every near-optimal hypothesis knows the model and does not reproduce the errors; at
λ/n = 10−4 , with the machine constant set to 0, it gives the parity’s label average probability at
least 0.983, holds at most 37.7 of the 11,710 bits of the error rule, and gives the erroneous label
average probability at most 0.199 on the error inputs. A few per cent below 2−20 and further
down, near-optimal hypotheses reproduce the errors. The theorem uses a list-decodable model
class, the product form of the error set, uniform inputs, an error density below 0.056, and a small
randomness deficiency of the error rule. Plain Bayes (Mprod , MF ), SI, and the λ-MAP under
every schedule with λ/n → 0 learn the errors, so λ must grow linearly in n, and the learner is
then deliberately not consistent for the teacher’s label distribution. That a bounded λ overfits
and consistency needs λ to grow is known for λ-weighted two-part MDL in noisy classification
(Grünwald and Langford, 2007; Zhu and Srebro, 2025; Zhu et al., 2026), where the target is the
data distribution rather than a norm that differs from it.
Limits. The trick sorts components by rate, not by validity. Density and description length do
not suffice: with Hänni’s density and an error set made of a cheap subcube of 128 points plus
896 random points (about 10,400 bits), no hypothesis that fits no better than the good model
is near-optimal for λ/n < 1.6 · 10−5 , about 31% of the window’s logarithmic width (25% with
machine constant 30). This refutes the extension of Hänni’s example, which he called “somewhat
cherry-picked”, to every error rule with his density and description length; whether structured
error rules leave a window is open. A cheap, frequent error rule defeats the trick for all input
lengths above a threshold linear in the machine constant (rate blindness); λ cannot be chosen from
imitation data, and the window depends on the input law. Out of distribution, λ proportional to
n removes the constant lower bound of Proposition 7.1, which becomes 2−λ(K(Q,b)+O(1)) , but only
by sharpening the preference for the shortest hypotheses that fit the training data; no guarantee
about unseen inputs follows.
The model–randomness split. Hänni also asked for “a preference for a simple sampler over
a model where the sampled bits are filled in” and noted that Solomonoff induction has none,
“both get similar posterior” (Hänni, 2026c). At λ = 1 this is confirmed with explicit factors
for every computable sampler ν with a program of length k: in SFI the posterior odds of the
∗
deterministic fill-ins against the sampler lie in [2−2 log(tn +1)−O(1) , 2k+δ ], with tn doubly logarithmic
in the input length and δ ∗ a Ville deficiency with Pr[δ ∗ ≥ j] ≤ 2−j , whatever the amount of
sampled randomness; this covers non-dyadic samplers such as the Bern(1/3) threshold hypotheses
of Eisenstat’s example (Definition 6.10). SI behaves the same up to 2O(k+K(n)) . For every fixed
λ > 1 in every countable product class, the sampler wins: every λ-MAP has length at most k +
∗
(k +δ ∗ )/(λ−1), the fill-ins’ posterior is at most 2λk+λδ −(λ−1)(− log ν(Dn )) , and with infinitely many
distinct inputs the λ-MAPs are eventually equivalent to ν on the inputs seen (summable Hellinger
distances, not equality). With shared randomness, or in SI, the universal mixture wins instead:
no sampler with (λ − 1)k > λℓU + O(1) is ever selected. In the language of algorithmic statistics,
for a noisy random parity on the full truth table the set (shared-randomness) sophistication is
O(log d), so the parity’s parameter counts as noise, while the product sophistication is d±O(log d)
for every flip rate below 1/4: the product structure function has the kink at the simple model
that Hänni anticipated, where with shared randomness “1 bit pays for 1 bit”.

48

11

Related work

Solomonoff’s operator induction. Solomonoff (2008) describes three kinds of induction; the
third concerns “an unordered set of ordered pairs”, where one predicts the second element of a new
pair from the first (we have seen only the abstract). Secondary sources describe its prediction as
j Q
a mixture of computable conditional models weighted by 2−|O | i Oj (ai | qi ), with a convergence
theorem bounding the total error by ln 2 times the complexity of the source (Özkural, 2015,
2017). Read this way it is the per-query mixture Mpq , which equals Mprod on distinct inputs
(Remark 3.2); x-only dominance on distinct inputs is then Solomonoff’s, and Mpq is the natural
object for fresh label noise (Proposition 4.7). SFI differs in sharing randomness across inputs
(Proposition 3.7).
Hutter’s chronological semimeasures. With inputs as actions and labels as percepts, SFIhist
is a universal chronological semimeasure in the sense of AIXI (Hutter, 2000, 2005) (Corollary 5.18);
that it agrees with Hutter’s construction up to constant factors is expected but was not checked
against his texts; Hutter lists supervised learning from examples among AIXI’s problem classes
(Hutter, 2007) (Remark 4.17). Lattimore et al. (2011) show, according to their abstract, that normalised Solomonoff induction learns computable sub-patterns of otherwise unstructured sequences
while the unnormalised version can fail; this bears on SI applied to x1 y1 x2 y2 · · · (Proposition 5.22).
Wood et al. (2013) state, in their abstract, that Solomonoff’s and Levin’s constructions define the
same class of priors for sequences; Theorem 5.17 is the corresponding statement for chronological
semimeasures. The observation that a history-aware universal mixture is the natural ideal learner
from examples is therefore prior work in substance.
Universal priors for classification. Lattimore and Hutter (2013) use algorithmic information
theory to argue against no-free-lunch pessimism and give a Solomonoff-inspired off-line classifier;
whether their random-selection result coincides with Proposition 4.10 we have not checked. Poland
and Hutter (2005) show that two-part MDL prediction can have loss bounds exponentially worse
than Bayes mixtures; the slides compare this with the gap between Occam and weighted majority
(Remark 8.15). Leike and Hutter (2015a) show that AIXI’s optimality depends on the reference
machine; Proposition 7.4 is a supervised analogue. Ryabko (2006) studies deterministic labels
with conditionally independent objects.
Learning theory. The guarantees of Section 4 are of the Occam (Blumer et al., 1987), PACBayes (McAllester, 1999), MDL (Barron and Cover, 1991; Grünwald, 2007), halving and weightedmajority (Littlestone, 1988; Littlestone and Warmuth, 1994), expert-advice (Vovk, 1990; CesaBianchi and Lugosi, 2006) and online-to-batch (Littlestone, 1989; Cesa-Bianchi et al., 2004)
kinds; Proposition 4.15 makes the comparison. Conformal prediction (Vovk et al., 2005) is an
exchangeability-based supervised framework related to Proposition 4.10; Vovk’s earlier universal forecasting algorithms (Vovk, 1992) we have not examined. Inductive inference of recursive
functions (Gold, 1967; Barzdin and Freivalds, 1972; Case and Smith, 1983) is the deterministic,
identification-in-the-limit ancestor, mostly for inputs in the order 0, 1, 2, . . . .
Computable versions and networks. Speed priors (Schmidhuber, 2002; Filan et al., 2016)
are time-bounded universal priors for sequences; Section 8 gives function versions. The simplicitybias literature on neural networks is discussed in Remark 9.12, and the steeper-prior material,
with its relation to λ-weighted MDL, in Section 10.
49

Remark 11.1 (what exists and what appears new). [known (snippets; unverified)] context §6; history §4.6;
foundations §6.6 At the level of a search that saw abstracts and secondary sources only, the following exist: the per-query product mixture for supervised pairs with its convergence theorem
(operator induction, as secondary sources describe it); the universal chronological semimeasure;
Solomonoff-style classifiers and discrete MDL for classification; Occam, PAC-Bayes and onlineto-batch bounds, including the exchangeability argument of Proposition 4.10; the halving bound
and its contrast with argmax selection on the same prior (the slides); the Walsh diagonalisation
and dimension bound for kernels and the vanishing odd eigenvalues of bias-free ReLU kernels
(Theorem 9.7 and Remark 9.9(a)); machine dependence of universal priors; and the conjunction
ladder (the slides). We found no prior use of the name “Solomonoff function induction” and
none of the following: the function-machine form of the universal chronological semimeasure, the
separations between SFI and SFIhist , and the comparisons with SI on the interleaved sequence
(Section 5); the OOD results of Section 7; the length-activated, round-clocked and history-aware
time-bounded mixtures (Section 8.1); the every-ordering form of circuit Bayes with explicit constants (Theorem 8.10; the statement itself is Hänni’s claim); the uniform-circuit padding bound
(Proposition 9.5); and the GP-classification and anti-correlation statements of Remark 9.9(b),(c).
Each of these should be checked against Solomonoff (2008), Hutter (2005) and Lattimore and
Hutter (2013) before any claim of novelty.

12

Discussion

SFI as ideal supervised learning. The positive results are what one asks of an ideal learner,
and they need no assumption on how the inputs are chosen. SFI’s cumulative log loss is within the
complexity of any computable random labeller on every consistent data sequence (Theorem 4.1);
when the labels come from a computable random function and the inputs do not depend on
unrevealed labels, its predictions merge with the true ones (Theorem 4.3); and with i.i.d. inputs
the averaged predictor has risk O(K/n) (Theorem 4.5). These guarantees are relative: they
compare SFI with computable labellers, in log loss, and they say nothing about which labeller the
data come from. The out-of-distribution results have the same character (Remarks 7.9 and 7.14).
On inputs that a simple property separates from the training data, SFI keeps a probability
bounded below for every simple behaviour, whatever the amount of data and whatever the true
labels; how large that probability is depends on the universal machine (Propositions 7.1 and 7.4).
In exchange, for a computable target f the total posterior error over any sequence of distinct
inputs is at most (ln 2)(K(f ) + c) (Theorem 7.7). The limits listed in Remark 4.16—log loss,
averaged predictors, function semantics, computability, machine constants, distribution shift and
the comparator class—are each a precise statement, not a caveat.
What the hypotheses may see. The large gaps between SFI and Solomonoff induction in
Sections 5 and 6 are properties of order-free prediction rather than of SFI. Every order-independent
predictor pays them (Propositions 5.5, 6.5 and 6.16), and hypotheses that see the list of previous
inputs remove them (Corollary 5.18, Proposition 6.7, and Theorem 6.19). Which of SFI and
SFIhist is the right ideal therefore depends on whether the order of the data carries information
about the labels. With i.i.d. inputs it does not, and SFI’s guarantees apply as stated. When a
teacher chooses the order, it can carry almost all of the information, as in the cyclic permutation,
where the labels repeat the inputs. The set of previous inputs is not enough to recover this
information (Propositions 5.19 and 6.8), and even with the list, “as good as Solomonoff induction”

50

holds against SI’s joint loss, not automatically against its conditional label loss (Theorem 5.20
and Proposition 5.21).
Which version to call SFI. The versions of Section 3 agree up to K(dom D) + O(1) bits,
and their gaps, although unbounded, are small on the known witnesses: about log d for the single
shortest program on two points with d-bit inputs, and a padded tape-length cost for the program
mixture (Corollary 3.10 and Proposition 3.6). For deterministic computable targets all of them
learn at the same rate (Remark 3.13 and Proposition 4.15), so the choice matters mostly for
random labels and for code lengths. There is no coding theorem for function programs (Theorem 3.9): the single shortest consistent program, which is what minimum description length with
one hypothesis selects, and the Bayes mixture are different objects for functions, unlike for strings.
We call MF SFI because it is the object of Hänni (2026b), it has a posterior over functions, it
dominates every machine law with constant 2−K(ν) , it is invariant, and its semimeasure-convention
loss does not depend on the order of the data (Remark 2.19). Where the randomness lives also
matters for noise: a random function cannot produce fresh noise on a repeated input, while a
per-query mixture can (Propositions 4.6 and 4.7). The companion paper shows that it matters
for steeper priors as well (Section 10).
Computable versions. The same structure appears with bounded resources. Time-bounded
mixtures keep constant regret against all predictors that see only the input and run within a
time bound (Proposition 8.4 and Corollary 8.5). For circuits, Bayes mistakes and circuit size are
polynomially related on every ordering (Theorem 8.10), while the simplest consistent hypothesis
can fail exponentially out of distribution (Theorem 8.14): the selection rule matters more than
the prior (Remark 8.15). For neural networks, the parameter–function map alone implies no
simplicity bias (Proposition 9.4); any bridge to circuit SFI must come from the architecture and
the prior, which is what the slides of Vaintrob and Hänni (2026b) claim for the strong-prior limit
(Remark 9.10).
Relation to the literature. As secondary sources describe it, Solomonoff’s operator induction
is the per-query mixture Mpq , which equals the product mixture on distinct inputs; SFI differs
from it by sharing randomness across inputs (Remark 3.2 and Proposition 3.7). The historyaware version is, in substance, Hutter’s universal chronological semimeasure (we have not checked
the identification against his texts), and Hutter lists supervised learning among AIXI’s problem
classes (Remark 4.17). The learning theorems are of the Occam, PAC-Bayes and online-to-batch
kinds, with K in place of log |H| (Proposition 4.15). Section 11 gives details. Our literature search
reached abstracts and secondary sources only, so we claim no novelty for anything listed there as
existing (Remark 11.1).
Open problems.

Collected from the sections.

• Foundations. Is every lower-semicomputable law of a random partial function dominated by
MF (Section 2.3)? Is there a two-sided characterisation of − log MF (D) by standard complexities (Section 2.5)? What is the arithmetical level of SFI’s conditionals (Section 2.5)? Is the
normalised loss of MF order-dependent, and which semimeasure-only lower bounds hold for it
(Section 2.4)?
• Versions. Is log(MF /W ) = O(K(d, |D|)), and what are the exact rates of the gaps of Table 2 (Section 3.4)? Does any computable family of data sets exhibit an unbounded gap
51

(Section 3.3)?
• Learning. Does the last iterate, not only the average, have expected risk O(K/n) (Remark 4.9)?
Is the leave-one-out error of SFI O(KF (f |B )/|B|) up to logarithms (Conjecture 4.12)?
• History. Can the K(n)+K(A) term of Corollary 5.14 be removed, and does the theorem extend
to defective samplers and infinite alphabets (Remark D.6)? Can K(∆f ) be replaced by an
algorithmic mutual information (Conjecture 5.15)? How does SFIhist compare with normalised
Solomonoff induction’s label loss, and do the separations of Proposition 5.19 and Theorem 5.26
hold normalised (Section 5.6)?
• Examples. The normalised loss over the full cyclic permutation and in Eisenstat’s example
(Section 6.3); the depth count for weak semimeasures (Section 6.2); SI’s label loss with raw
undelimited blocks (Conjecture E.5).
• Out of distribution. A positive statement for properties that are simple given a program fitted
to the training data (Section 7.4); a version of “natural machines generalise as intended” for a
fixed reference machine (Conjecture 7.17).
• Computable versions and networks. Efficient constant regret against time-bounded samplers
(Section 8.1); a circuit coding theorem for size priors with polynomial tails, and Occam failures
beyond conjunctions (Section 8.3); the upper half of the uniform-circuit bridge, a lower bound
on the network log prior, and a check of the slides’ main theorem (Section 9).
The companion paper. Hänni (2026a) asks what a steeper simplicity prior does in function
induction with private randomness per input. In a window of λ/n it keeps a learner from reproducing a teacher’s incompressible systematic errors, and for fixed λ > 1 it prefers a simple
sampler to a model with filled-in random bits; with shared randomness, or in sequence prediction,
a universal hypothesis absorbs everything instead (Section 10). Whether structured error rules
leave such a window is open there.

The coin-flip example of Section 6.3 is due to Sam Eisenstat. Section 9 and the conjunction ladder
of Theorem 8.14 build on joint slides with Dmitry Vaintrob (Vaintrob and Hänni, 2026b).
This paper was developed and written with extensive assistance from Claude (Anthropic),
an AI system. Working from the author’s notes (Hänni, 2026b) and under his direction, Claude
produced the proofs, the computations and the text. The results were developed in five groups.
Each group’s notes, with full proofs and seeded scripts, were checked by a separate instance of the
system acting as an adversarial referee, which re-derived the proofs and wrote independent code;
the notes were then revised, and the paper was written from the revised notes. No human has yet
independently checked all of the mathematics, and readers should weigh the results accordingly.
The literature was searched only through a web-search tool that returned excerpts; most cited
works were not read in full, and the text says so where it relies on them.

Code and data
Every computation reported here was done by a Python script with a fixed seed or by a deterministic one. The scripts, their saved outputs, and the research notes with full proofs are in the folder
52

solomonoff-function-induction/ of the repository https://github.com/kaarelh/AI-works:
the scripts are in research/tracks/*/checks/, research/tracks/*/referee_code/ and code/.
Each script saves its output next to itself, and re-running the scripts reproduces the saved outputs,
apart from timing lines and one time-limited optimisation run.

Notation is that of Section 2. Tapes are ω = ω1 ω2 · · · ; a finite union of cylinders [w] is identified

Proof of Proposition 2.3. (a) Along any order the conditionals telescope to ν(D)/ν(∅) = ν(D); if
an intermediate value vanishes, so does ν(D), by monotonicity. They sum to at most 1 by the
weak inequality. (b) The empty product is 1. Revealing D first and x last gives νq (D + (x, y)) =
νq (D) q(y | D, x); summing over y gives the weak inequality, and dividing gives the formula for
q.

every conditional of an order-independent predictor q (Proposition 2.3) sums to 1, then νq is the
containment functional of a unique law of a random total function F ∈ Y X .

Y is countable, so Kolmogorov’s extension theorem gives a law on Y X ; it is unique because its

A.2

Machine laws and filling-in processes

Proof of Proposition 2.8. Each coordinate of fωT is determined by countably many cylinders, so
S
ω 7→ fωT is measurable into Y⊥X . The set {ω : fωT (x) = y} = (x,w,y)∈T [w] is effectively open,
uniformly in (x, y) and an index of T ; a finite intersection of effectively open sets is effectively
open uniformly; and the measure of an effectively open set is lsc uniformly in its description.
{fω ⊇ ∅} is the whole space.
Enumeration. Let (Wi ) enumerate all c.e. sets of triples. Let Ti be obtained by enumerating
Wi and accepting each triple unless it conflicts (same input, comparable strings, different labels)
with a triple already accepted. Each Ti is c.e. and consistent, and Ti = Wi whenever Wi is
consistent. So (Ti ) lists exactly the function machines.
58

Proof of Theorem 2.9. TU is c.e. If (x, pw, y), (x, p′ w′ , y ′ ) ∈ TU with pw, p′ w′ comparable, then
p, p′ are comparable elements of the prefix-free set dom V , so p = p′ , and (x, w, y), (x, w′ , y ′ ) are
triples of the consistent TV (p) with w, w′ comparable; so y = y ′ . For ω = pω ′ with p ∈ dom V ,
T

fωTU = fω′V (p) , and ω ′ is uniform and independent of p. A tape with no prefix in dom V gives the
empty function, which extends only ∅. Summing over p gives (1), and m(i) ≥ 2−K(i) gives the
bound.
Lemma A.2 (Laws of random total functions). [proved] foundations Lemma 6.5a For countable Y , every
P
lsc law ν of a random total function (ν(∅) = 1, ν(D) = y ν(D +(x, y))) is computable, uniformly
in an index. So the machine laws of random total functions are exactly the computable laws of
random total functions.
Proof of Lemma A.2. Induction on |D|. ν(∅) = 1 is computable. If ν(D) is computable, then
P
ν(D + (x, y)) = ν(D) − y′ ̸=y ν(D + (x, y ′ )) is a computable real minus an lsc (possibly infinite)
sum, hence upper semicomputable; it is also lsc, hence computable. The induction is uniform.
The converse is Proposition 2.10(c), last clause.
Corollary A.3 (Universality among normalised order-independent predictors). [proved] context Cor
5.2(c), extended by foundations Lemma 6.5a For countable Y : if ν is lsc and weak, ν(∅) = 1, and all its
conditionals sum to 1, then MF ≥ 2−K(ν)−O(1) ν.
Proof. By Proposition A.1, ν is the law of a random total function, hence computable
(Lemma A.2), hence a machine law whose index is computable from that of ν (Proposition 2.10(c)); apply Theorem 2.9.
So order-independence alone gives the weak semimeasures, where M w is universal (Theorem 2.12); adding normalised predictions gives the random total functions, where MF is universal.
Remark A.4 (Computable filling-in processes). [proved] foundations Rem 2.7 A computable filling-in
process is a Markov chain F0 = ∅ ⊆ F1 ⊆ F2 ⊆ · · · of finite partial functions whose transition
kernel Pr[Ft+1 = g | Ft = f ] is supported on finitely many extensions g ⊇ f , given as an explicit
S
list with rational probabilities computable from (t, f ). The law of its limit F∞ := t Ft is a
machine law, with an index computable from the kernel. Conversely, every machine law is the
law of the limit of the nested process Ft := “the part of fω certified by triples enumerated within
t steps with |w| ≤ t”, which depends on ω1:t ; this process is Markov in ω1:t , not in Ft . So machine
laws are exactly the limits of computable nested processes driven by a hidden finite state.
Proof. The machine reads the tape bit by bit and samples F1 , F2 , . . . in turn: to sample from a
finitely supported law with rational probabilities it reads bits until the dyadic interval named by
the bits read so far lies inside one of the consecutive half-open intervals whose lengths are those
probabilities, and takes that outcome. This fails to terminate only when the tape’s real is an
endpoint, a null set. On query x it runs the process until the first t with x ∈ dom Ft and outputs
Ft (x); it diverges if there is none. So T contains (x, w, y) when the process, run on the bits of w,
produces F1 , . . . , Ft with t the first index such that x ∈ dom Ft , and Ft (x) = y. The process reads
its bits in order, so on comparable strings it produces the same initial segment of the chain; with
Ft ⊆ Ft+1 this makes T consistent. Off the null set the sampled chain has the law of the process,
and fω = F∞ . The converse is a restatement of Definition 2.7.

A.6

The open question on random-function laws

Reformulation (proof sketch; foundations Problem 3.6). Suppose an lsc νP has approximations νPt ↑ νP , where each Pt is a law of a random partial function on a finite domain with
finitely many labels and rational probabilities, computable from t, and consecutive laws admit a
coupling with Ft ⊆ Ft+1 . The couplings form a polytope given by rational linear constraints, so a
rational coupling can be found by search, and Remark A.4 then realises νP exactly as a machine
law. By Strassen’s theorem for finite partially ordered sets (Strassen, 1965) (quoted, not checked
against the source), such a coupling exists if and only if Pt (U ) ≤ Pt+1 (U ) for every up-set U of the
extension order. The containment events {F ⊇ D} are only the principal up-sets, so νPt ≤ νPt+1
does not give this. The question becomes whether every lsc νP admits, at a constant-factor loss,
approximations that increase on all up-sets.
Two steps are not written out: the Strassen step is quoted, and the reduction from general
lower approximations of νP to finite rational laws Pt is not carried out. An adversary argument in
the spirit of Example 2.6, forcing repeated couplings on fresh blocks, was tried and settled neither
direction.

A.7

The normalised convention

Proposition A.6 (Two-point criterion). [proved; computed] foundations Prop 5.4 (= its referee’s Prop B);
checks c10(a), r13 Let P be the law of a random partial function on inputs a, b, with A := {F (a) ̸= ⊥},
B := {F (b) ̸= ⊥} and P (A), P (B) > 0. Consider
(⋆)

P (B | F (a) = α) = P (B | A)

and

P (A | F (b) = β) = P (A | B)

for all α, β of positive probability. (i) If (⋆) holds, the normalised probability of {(a, α), (b, β)}
is the same in both reveal orders, for every (α, β). (ii) Conversely, if every two-point probability
P (F (a) = α, F (b) = β), α, β ∈ Y , is positive (as for MF ) and the two orders agree for every
(α, β), then (⋆) holds. (iii) Without positivity (ii) can fail.
Proof. Put p(α, β) := P (F (a) = α, F (b) = β), u(α) := P (B | F (a) = α)/P (B) and v(β) := P (A |
F (b) = β)/P (A). Revealing a first gives the normalised product
P (F (a) = α)
p(α, β)
p(α, β)
·
=

P (A)
P (F (a) = α, B)
P (A)P (B) u(α)

and revealing b first gives p(α, β)/(P (A)P (B) v(β)). When p(α, β) > 0 the two agree exactly
when u(α) = v(β); when p(α, β) = 0 both are 0. (i) (⋆) gives u ≡ P (A ∩ B)/(P (A)P (B)) ≡ v.
(ii) Agreement on all pairs forces u(α) = v(β) for all α, β, so u ≡ v ≡ γ for a constant γ.
Averaging u over α with weights P (F (a) = α)/P (A) gives γ = P (A ∩ B)/(P (A)P (B)), which is
(⋆). (iii) The law with (F (a), F (b)) equal to (0, 0) with probability 41 , (1, 1), (1, ⊥), (⊥, 1) with
probability 81 each, and (⊥, ⊥) with probability 38 has P (B | F (a) = 0) = 1, P (B | F (a) = 1) = 21 ,
P (B | A) = 34 ; it puts positive mass only on the diagonal defined pairs and is symmetric in a, b,
so its normalised probabilities are order-independent (c10(a4)).
The law of Proposition 2.17(b) violates (⋆): P (B | F (a) = 0) = 21 ̸= 23 = P (B | A). Total laws
always satisfy it. Check c10 found agreement between (⋆) and order independence on 400 of 400
random laws with positive two-point cells (r13: 300 of 300).

Proof of Proposition A.7. (a) Each normalised factor is the raw factor divided by Zt . At the first
step Z1 = U{fω (x1 ) ̸= ⊥} ≥ 2−c , because a constant function is a machine law of complexity

A.8

Invariance, coding bounds, sequence induction, computability

Proof of Theorem 2.20. (a) Each of MF , MF′ is a machine law in the other’s enumeration, so each
dominates the other up to a constant factor by Theorem 2.9; the same argument applies to any
two function machines whose laws dominate all machine laws up to constants.
(b) α−1 and β −1 are partial computable (by search). The set of triples (α(x), w, β(y)) with
(x, w, y) ∈ TU is a function machine over X ′ , Y ′ (consistent because α is injective), whose random
function is β ◦ fω ◦ α−1 , undefined off the range of α. Its law ν ′ satisfies ν ′ (Dαβ ) = U{β(fω (x)) =
β(y) ∀(x, y) ∈ D} = MF (D), since β is injective. Its index is computable from indices of α, β, so
MF′ (Dαβ ) ≥ 2−K(α,β)−O(1) MF (D). Conversely, the machine with random function β −1 ◦ fω′ ◦ α
on X has law ν with ν(D) = MF′ (Dαβ ), so MF (D) ≥ 2−K(α,β)−O(1) MF′ (Dαβ ).
(c) For W : a program for φ becomes a program for β ◦ φ ◦ α−1 by a prefix of length K(α, β) +
O(1), and conversely. For Mprod : transform each conditional semimeasure in the same way. For
M w : given a weak ν on X × Y , put ν ′ (D′ ) := ν(D) if D′ = Dαβ and ν ′ (D′ ) := 0 otherwise; ν ′ is
lsc (search for preimages). If D′ = Dαβ and x′ ∈
/ dom D′ , the extensions of D′ at x′ of the form
63

E αβ have x′ = α(x) and labels β(y), so the one-point inequality transfers; if D′ is not an image,
neither is any extension of it. The converse transformation is symmetric.
Proof of Theorem 2.21. (a) P (E | S) := MF (E) for dom E = S is lsc uniformly in (E, S) and
sums to at most 1 over E (second consequence of Definition 2.2). The conditional coding theorem
gives K(E | S) ≤ − log MF (E) + O(1). Take E = D|T , S = T , and use MF (D) ≤ MF (D|T ).
(b) First term: the shortest consistent program p gives the machine law 1[φV (p) ⊇ D], whose
complexity is at most |p| + O(1), so MF (D) ≥ 2−KF (D)−O(1) (equivalently, MF ≥ 2−O(1) W by
P
Theorem 3.4). Second term: m(y | x) := q:V (q,x)=y 2−|q| , with V (·, x) the conditional universal
prefix machine, is an lsc conditional semimeasure with m(y | x) ≥ 2−K(y|x) ; its product law is a
machine law of complexity O(1) (Proposition 2.10(a)). Last inequality: KF (D) ≤ K(D) + O(1)
(a program computes the lookup table of D from a shortest description of D) and K(D) ≤
K(dom D) + K(D | dom D) + O(1).
(c) Combine (a) with T = {x} and (b).
Proof of Proposition 2.23. (≥) Let T1 contain (i, w, b) when the universal monotone machine, run
on w, has printed more than i bits and its bit i + 1 is b. Outputs on comparable strings are
comparable, so T1 is a function machine, and νT1 (Dx ) = M (x); apply Theorem 2.9. (≤) The
monotone machine that prints fω (0), fω (1), . . . in order, as long as each value is certified along
ω and lies in {0, 1}, prints a string extending x exactly when fω ⊇ Dx . So x 7→ MF (Dx ) is the
output semimeasure of a monotone machine, and M dominates it (Zvonkin and Levin, 1970).
Proof of Proposition 2.24. (a) Proposition 2.8 and the remark after Definition 2.15.
(c) Take X = N and suppose the raw conditional is computable. Define f by recursion:
with Dt := {(s, f (s)) : s < t}, let f (t) be the first y ∈ {0, 1} for which approximation certifies
MF (y | Dt , t) < 0.6. The two conditionals sum to at most 1, so one of them is at most 21 ,
Q
and the search halts. f is total computable, and MF (Dn ) = t<n MF (f (t) | Dt , t) < 0.6n ,
which contradicts MF (Dn ) ≥ 2−K(f )−O(1) for large n. For the normalised conditional the same
construction gives normalised loss at least n log(1/0.6), while it is at most LSFI (Dn ) ≤ K(f )+O(1)
(Proposition 2.17(a)).
(b) If MF were computable, the raw conditional would be a ratio of positive computable reals,
hence computable.
(d) Lsc reals are computable relative to ∅′ ; the conditionals are ratios of positive lsc reals
(MF (D) and, for the normalised one, Z(D, x)).

B

Kolmogorov and semimeasure versions: proofs

Notation is that of Section 3. j(x) is the length-lexicographic index of x ∈ {0, 1}∗ , so j(x) ≤
2d+1 − 2 for |x| ≤ d.

B.1

The variants and the chain

Remark B.1 (Why W ’s programs delimit themselves before reading the input). [proved] foundations
remark after Def 4.1; context §1.1 With this choice W is the containment functional of the random partial
function “draw p with probability 2−|p| , output φV (p) ”: a mixture of fixed functions with inputindependent weights. Summing 2−|p| over the programs of a conditional prefix machine V (p, x)
is a different object: for each x the halting programs form a prefix-free set, but their union
over x need not, so the weights define no prior over functions. Reading a conditional program
64

lazily from one shared tape does give a random function, the function machine x 7→ V (ω, x); it
is a machine law of complexity O(1), not a mixture of fixed functions, and on single points it
gives 2−K(y|x)±O(1) (Theorem 2.21(c)). The two notions of conditional complexity are close: with
K P (z | x) := min{|h| : h ∈ dom V, φV (h) (x) = z},
K(z | x) − c ≤ K P (z | x) ≤ K(z | x) + K(K(z | x)) + c,
since every h ∈ dom V is a conditional program, and a conditional program prefixed with a
self-delimiting code of its length delimits itself without reading x.
Proof of Theorem 3.4. (1) The shortest consistent program is one term of W . (2) Pe (y | x) :=
1[φe (x) = y] is a conditional semimeasure, lsc uniformly in e; let g be computable with Pg(e) =
P
P
Pe . Then i 7→ e:g(e)=i m(e) is an lsc discrete semimeasure, so m(i) ≥ 2−O(1) e:g(e)=i m(e),
P
and Mprod (D) ≥ 2−O(1) e m(e)1[φe ⊇ D] = 2−O(1) W (D). (3) Mprod is an lsc mixture of
product laws, a machine law (Proposition 2.10(a,d)); apply Theorem 2.9. (4) Theorem 2.13(i).
(5) KF (D) ≤ K(D) + O(1) (lookup table), K(D) ≤ K(dom D) + K(D | dom D) + O(1), and
K(D | dom D) ≤ − log M w (D) + O(1) by the conditional coding theorem applied to E 7→ M w (E)
on data sets with domain dom D, whose total is at most 1 (Definition 2.2).

B.5

No coding theorem; the covering bound
g

Proof of Theorem 3.9. (i) Let g := ℓd = ⌈log(d/ log 3)⌉ − 1, so that 32 < 2d (c8(a) checks the
arithmetic for all d ≤ 2·105 and along powers of 2 up to 2200 ). Fewer than 2g programs have length
less than g; let E be the set of their indices V (p). Colour x ∈ {0, 1}d by (χe (x))e∈E ∈ {0, 1, ∗}E
as in Proposition 3.5. There are at most 3|E| < 2d colours, so two distinct x1 , x2 share a colour,
and no e ∈ E has φe (x1 ) = 0, φe (x2 ) = 1. So KF (D) ≥ g ≥ log d − log log 3 − 1 > log d − 2.
(ii) Proposition 3.6(b) with |D| = 2. (iii) The uniform product law, as in Proposition 3.5.
For the last claim: by (i), log d ≤ KF (D) + 2, so ⌈log(d + 1)⌉ ≤ KF (D) + 4; and K(m) ≤
log m + 2 log log m + O(1) for m ≥ 2.
In terms of sets of programs: with SD := {e : φe ⊇ D}, W (D) = m(SD ) and KF (D) =
mine∈SD K(e). Levin’s bound on the lowest complexity of the elements of a set relates exactly
these two quantities to the information the set carries about the halting problem (Levin, 2016;
Epstein and Levin, 2011) [known; unverified]; we have not checked its statement, nor whether it
applies to infinite c.e. sets such as SD , and nothing here depends on it. For binary sequences,
monotone complexity and − log M also differ unboundedly (Gács, 1983; Day, 2011) (statements
not checked); we make no comparison of rates.
Proof of Corollary 3.10. Lower bound: Theorem 3.9(i),(iii). Upper bound: LSFI (D) ≥ 0, and
Proposition 3.11 with k = 2 gives KF (D) ≤ 2 + log(2(d + 2)) + K(⌈log(d + 2)⌉, 2) + O(1).
Proof of Proposition 3.11. Put s := ⌈log(d + 2)⌉ and d′ := 2s − 2 ≥ d; then d′ + 2 < 2(d + 2)
d′ +1

(c8(d)). There are at most 2 k −1 2k ≤ 2k(d +2) binary-labelled data sets of size k on {0, 1}≤d .

Put Ncov := ⌊2k k(d′ + 2) ln 2⌋ + 1. Ncov independent uniform truth tables on {0, 1}≤d all miss


−k

′

a fixed such D with probability (1 − 2−k )Ncov < e−Ncov 2 ≤ 2−k(d +2) . By the union bound
the expected number of uncovered data sets is below 1, so some family of Ncov truth tables
covers all of them. The lexicographically first such family is computable from (d′ , k), hence
from (s, k), by exhaustive search. A program consists of a V -program for (s, k) followed by
a ⌈log Ncov ⌉-bit index (its length is computable from (s, k), so the program is self-delimiting);

it outputs an index of the chosen truth table, undefined off {0, 1}≤d . Its length is at most

consequence, LSFI (D) ≥ K(D | dom D) − O(1) = k − δD − O(1) by Theorem 2.21(a).
Check c8(c) computes, for d ≤ 3 and k ≤ 3, the exact expected number of uncovered data sets
(< 1 in all nine cases) and finds that random families of size Ncov cover everything in at least two
thirds of the trials in every case. Explicit two-point coverings
also exist: the ⌊N/2⌋-subsets of
N −1 
{0, . . . , N −1} that contain 0, read as N functions on ⌊N/2⌋−1 inputs, show all four label patterns
on every pair of inputs (c8(b), N ≤ 14). This is the construction of Kleitman and Spencer (1973),
reported to be optimal [known; unverified]; we did not check optimality against the source, and the
checks confirm it only for N ≤ 6 (c8(b)) and N ≤ 8 (r11). Covering all inputs of length at most d
this way takes at most d + 6 functions for d ≤ 40 (c8(b)). Nothing above depends on optimality.

B.6

Structured outputs and history-aware SFI

Proposition B.4 (Single queries and structured outputs). [proved] context Prop 5.4(3),(4) Let M (· | x)
be the universal monotone semimeasure given x, over the alphabet {0, 1, $}: Solomonoff induction
predicting an output y symbol by symbol after x. Then 2−K(y|x) ≤ 2c M (y | x); but M (0d | ε) ≥ 2−c ,
d
while 2−K(0 ) = 2−K(d)±O(1) . With an end marker, M (y$ | x) = 2−K(y|x)±O(1) .
Since LSFI ({(x, y)}) = K(y | x) ± O(1) (Theorem 2.21(c)), single-query SFI treats an output
as a complete object: it commits to where the output ends and pays for its length, while sequence
induction on an undelimited output does not. With delimiters the two agree up to constants;
across many examples they differ in how information is shared (Section 5).
Proof of Proposition B.4. A conditional prefix program for y given x is a monotone program,
P
given x, that prints y; so M (y | x) ≥ 2−c q:V (q,x)=y 2−|q| ≥ 2−K(y|x)−c . A constant program
prints 0∞ , so M (0d | ε) ≥ 2−c , while K(0d ) = K(d) ± O(1). For the end marker, “≥” is the first
claim applied to y$, with K(y$ | x) = K(y | x) ± O(1). For “≤”: the strings y$, y ∈ {0, 1}∗ , are
P
pairwise incomparable, so their cylinders are disjoint and y M (y$ | x) ≤ 1; y 7→ M (y$ | x) is lsc
uniformly in x, and the conditional coding theorem gives M (y$ | x) ≤ 2−K(y|x)+c .

(4)

In proofs about ν ∈ {W, MF } we treat each hypothesis as a deterministic partial function: for W
a program p with weight 2−|p| and function φV (p) ; for MF a tape ω with measure U and function
fω . Then ν(D) is the mass of the hypotheses that extend D.

Identity of Remark 4.9. With xn+1 an independent sample, ER(Qn ) = E[− log Qn (φ(xn+1 ) |
xn+1 )]. SFI sees its data only as a set and (x1 , . . . , xn+1 ) is exchangeable, so the same expectation

Proof of Proposition 4.10. In every order the step losses sum to − log(ν(f |B )/ν(∅)) ≤ Lν (f |B )
(Proposition 2.16, ν(∅) ≤ 1). The split point is uniform and independent of the order; divide by
70

|B|. A shortest program consistent with f |B is a term of W (f |B ), so LW ≤ KF , and LMF ≤ LW +c
(Theorem 3.4). For the error use e ≤ − ln(1 − e).
Proof of Proposition 4.11. eLOO (x) ν(f |B\{x} ) = ν(f |B\{x} ) − ν(f |B ) is the mass of hypotheses
correct on B \ {x} and wrong or undefined at x. A hypothesis in the sets of two points x ̸= x′
would be correct and not correct at x′ , so the sets are disjoint, and none contains a hypothesis
correct on all of B. So the masses sum to at most ν(∅) − ν(f |B ). Divide by ν(f |B\{x} ) ≥ ν(f |B );
the last inequality is as in Proposition 4.10.

Proof of Proposition 4.13. (1) The prefix u depends only on x∗ , which is independent of the

independently, so the probability is (1 − 2−ℓ )n ≥ 1 − n2−ℓ ≥ 12 . (2) A decider needs u: a selfdelimiting code of ℓ, then ℓ bits. (3) On the event of (1), (5) with G = Gu and b ≡ 1 gives
ewr (x∗ ) ≥ 2−K(Gu )−c . As ℓ ≤ log n + 2, 2−ℓ ≥ 1/(4n) and (ℓ + 1)2 ≤ (log n + 3)2 . For computable
binary f take b = 1 − f , with K(b) ≤ K(f ) + c.

Proof of Proposition 4.15. Put εp := (|p| ln 2 + ln(1/δ))/n. A fixed p whose disagreement with φ
exceeds εp is consistent with Dn with probability at most (1 − εp )n ≤ e−nεp = 2−|p| δ. Sum over

The following proposition is stated here rather than in Section 4, for the page cap.
Proposition C.1 (chosen inputs). [proved] context Prop 5.3 (1) For every weak semimeasure ν and
every query set A, chosen adaptively or not, the total semimeasure-convention log loss on the
labels of A is − log(ν(f |A )/ν(∅)) in every order: for log loss, choosing the order is worthless. (2)
Small total loss does not give efficient identification. For fa (x) := 1[x = a] on {0, 1}d , SFI’s total
loss on any query sequence, even all of {0, 1}d , is at most K(fa ) + c ≤ d + 2 log d + c, yet every
query strategy, adaptive or randomised, needs at least (2d − 1)/2 queries in expectation over a
uniform a to identify a.
Proof. (1) is Proposition 2.16. (2) fa is computable from a and K(a) ≤ d + 2 log d + c; apply
Theorem 4.1(b). Fix the strategy’s coins. Until a is queried every answer is 0, so the queries are
those made on f ≡ 0. Identifying a requires querying a or all other 2d − 1 points, so at least
min(pos(a), 2d − 1) queries, where pos(a) is the time of the first query of a on f ≡ 0. These times

P2d −1
2d −1

are distinct, so their mean over uniform a is at least 2−d
k=1 k + (2 − 1) ≥
