over the coins.
So for active learning the log-loss guarantee must be paired with another objective, such as
expected information gain (Orseau et al., 2013) (content not checked).

C.7

Further remarks

Heuristic for Conjecture 4.12 (not proved). The natural one-exception hypotheses “f except at
P
x” have weight about 2−K(f )−K(x|f ) , and x 2−K(x|f ) ≤ 1. A counterexample needs many more
than K(f ) points x whose one-point flips f x have K(f x ) ≲ K(f ); natural attempts (halting-set
indicators, pseudo-random seeds, thresholds) give only O(1) or O(K(f )) such points.
Remark C.2 (learning curves can increase). [computed (context c3 Part 2)] context Rem 2.11 Take a
Bayes mixture of the truth f (weight wf ) and |B| hypotheses each wrong at exactly one point of
w +w(|B|−k+1)
B (weight w each). Sampling without replacement, the loss at step k is log wf f +w(|B|−k) in every
order, and it increases with k. With w = wf = 1/(|B| + 1) and |B| = 16, the last step (leaveone-out) costs 1 bit, while the random-split average is 0.255 bits. Each leave-one-out error is 12 ,
within a factor of about 2 of Proposition 4.11. By Kraft’s inequality such weights w = wf = 2−K
need |B| ≤ 2K − 1.
Remark C.3 (a refuted comparison). [refuted] context Prop 2.12, X13 An earlier version of the context
notes read Proposition 4.13 as “matching the O(1/n) Cesàro upper bound up to log2 n”. The
comparison is invalid: it sets a per-step lower bound against an average over steps, which says
P
nothing about the per-step rate. Indeed k Eek ≤ (ln 2)(K(f ) + c), so lim inf k k Eek = 0. The
valid per-step comparison is Corollary 4.14.
Remark C.4 (relations among the in-distribution bounds). [remark]
P
context Rem 2.18 (i) For computable f and distinct inputs,
k≤n ek ≤ (ln 2)(K(f ) + c) on every
sequence (Theorem 4.1(b) with e ≤ − ln(1 − e); this is Theorem 7.7). Corollary 4.14(a) gives a
per-step rate in distribution at the price of an additive ln n; Theorem 4.5(a) is the Cesàro statement
72

for the log loss of the averaged predictor. (ii) Corollary 4.14(a) is the Markov half of the relevancebased bound in the Vaintrob–Hänni slides (Vaintrob and Hänni, 2026b, Appendix A), specialised
to W and MF . The union-bound half and the slides’ geometric length prior are not needed: long
programs enter only multiplied by e−εn . (iii) Leave-one-out is different. For a fixed finite B and
a random held-out point, the training set eliminates every hypothesis with two or more errors on
B; what remains is the mass of one-exception hypotheses (Conjecture 4.12). If Conjecture 4.12
holds, the proof of Corollary 4.14(b) gives Ee(x∗ ) = O(K(f )/(n + 1)) up to logarithmic factors,
without the additive ln n.
Comparison with other bounds (foundations §6.6; the cited works were not checked against
their sources). Occam (Blumer et al., 1987): Proposition 4.15 is a description-length
form of the
p
Occam bound. PAC-Bayes (McAllester, 1999): risk bounds with a KL(Q∥prior)/n term, or
a KL /n term in the realisable case; with prior 2−K and a posterior concentrated on the target
the term is about K(φ) ln 2/n, the same rate. The difference is in kind: PAC-Bayes bounds hold
with high probability for a randomised classifier, Theorem 4.5 in expectation for the averaged
Bayes predictive. MDL and the index of resolvability (Barron and Cover, 1991): Theorem 4.5(b)
and Proposition 4.7(c) are Cesàro-average resolvability bounds minP ′ [K(P ′ )/n + KL(P ∥P ′ )] in
the conditional setting, for every input distribution. Expert advice (Vovk, 1990; Cesa-Bianchi
and Lugosi, 2006): under log loss a Bayes mixture has regret log(1/wi ) against expert i on every
sequence; Theorem 4.1 is this fact with machine laws as experts, each predicting with its own
posterior given past labels, so shared randomness is allowed.
Remark C.5 (positive-example induction). [proof sketch; known (snippet)] context Rem 5.5 Hänni lists
“positive example induction”, learning a set or a distribution from samples, among the ideal
inductions he wants to define (Hänni, 2026b). In SFI terms positive labels (x, 1) alone are useless,
because the constant-1 program explains them. The right formalisation is a sampling likelihood:
the samples are the labels of the index inputs i 7→ xi , under index-ignoring hypotheses with private
P
Q
randomness per sample. The mixture is then P wP i P (xi ) over computable distributions P
on X, the private-randomness analogue of the universal semimeasure on the sample sequence. It
appears to be the object of the ideal learner from positive evidence of Chater and Vitányi (2007);
their exact model was not checked.
Remark C.6 (interventions). [remark]

context Rem 5.6 If the inputs are interventions set by the experimenter independently of the system’s

randomness, x-only dominance (Theorem 4.1(a)) applies to the interventional law P (y | do(x)).
With observational inputs SFI targets P (y | x) like any conditional learner. Nothing specific to
SFI is proved here.

Computations. All scripts are deterministic or seeded and store their output next to themselves; the numbers quoted in Section 4 and here are taken from these outputs. Foundations: c4
(dominance, order independence and the worst adaptive rule on finite mixtures over four inputs),
r6 (a rule that sees F ), c5 (seed 20261011; fresh noise), c7 and r7 (the per-query mixture on a
class of 133 laws), r9 and r12 (exact finite analogues of Theorem 4.5(a) and of the last iterate),
c11 (seed 20261012; Proposition 4.8, the identity of Remark 4.9 to 1.6·10−15 , and the integrability
inequality, largest value 0.51 < log e). Context: c3 (seed 2; random splits and Remark C.2), c4
(seed 3; separating prefixes at d = 48, n ≤ 104 , matching the exact probabilities), c7 (seed 7;
Proposition 4.11 on all 31 subsets of a five-point domain and Corollary 4.14 exactly for n ≤ 6).

Proofs for history-aware prediction

Notation is that of Section 5. We use: (F1) Theorem 2.9 for any countable decidable input set,
MF (D) ≥ 2−K(T ) νT (D) for D ̸= ∅, and MF (D) ≥ 2−K(g)−c if a partial computable g extends
D (Proposition 2.10(b)); (F2) the conditional coding theorem of Section 2.1: if ν(z | w) is
P
lsc uniformly in w and z ν(z | w) ≤ 1, then K(z | w) ≤ − log ν(z | w) + K(ν) + c; (F3)
Levin–Schnorr: ω is Martin-Löf random iff K(ω1:ℓ ) ≥ ℓ − cω for all ℓ (Li and Vitányi, 2008);
(F4) K(a, b) ≤ K(b) + K(a | b) + c and K(s) ≤ |s| + K(|s|) + c (Li and Vitányi, 2008); (F5)
M ≥ 2−K(ν)−c ν for every lsc semimeasure ν on {0, 1}∗ (Zvonkin and Levin, 1970; Li and Vitányi,
2008); (F6) Sion’s minimax theorem (Sion, 1958), for compact convex sets in Rm and a continuous
payoff convex in one variable and affine in the other.

Proof of Lemma 5.2. The events for different next labels are disjoint subsets of the event for y1:n ,
and at a repeated input only the stored label survives; lower semicomputability is as for MF .

input the chronological inequality is the weak inequality, and at a repeated input only one term
is nonzero.

Lemma D.3 (Private memory). [proved] history Lemma 3.0 Let a reveal mechanism that does not
see f consist of a random element Γ (private memory and coins) independent of f and maps
xi = seli (Γ, hi−1 ), with reveal-sequence law ∆(x1:n ∥y1:n−1 ) := P[seli (Γ, hi−1 ) = xi ∀i ≤ n]. (a)
Given hi−1 , f and xi are conditionally independent; so I(f → x1:n ) = 0 for finite-valued inputs
and labels. (b) Given f , the input sequence has probability ∆(x1:n ∥f (x1 ), . . . , f (xn−1 )), and the
effective kernel ∆(x1:i ∥y1:i−1 )/∆(x1:i−1 ∥y1:i−2 ) depends neither on f nor on its law. (c) If the
mechanism is a computable sampler with private memory that halts with probability 1 at each step
whatever labels it is fed, and its inputs up to time n lie in a finite listed set An , then ∆(x1:n ∥y1:n−1 )
is a computable real, uniformly in its arguments.

f -factor does not involve xi . (b) Sum the Γ-factor over Γ. (c) ∆(x∥y) is lsc (enumerate halting

x′1 ∼ ∆(· | ∅), y1′ ∼ P (· | ∅, x′1 ), x′2 ∼ ∆(· | h′1 ), . . . , each call reading fresh consecutive bits

with x′j = x and outputs yj′ , diverging if there is none. Its answer depends on a finite prefix of ω,
so T is a function machine, with K(T ) ≤ K(∆, P ) + c. If the trajectory begins with x1 y1 · · · xn yn ,
each x ∈ dom Dn is first visited with its label in Dn (the sequence is consistent), so fωT ⊇ Dn . The

block i − 1 if they agree and i ≥ 2, and ϵ otherwise. Given hi−1 , xi is uniform on {0, 1}i ; given f it
is determined; so I(f ; xi | hi−1 ) = i. The losses are Proposition 5.5(c) and Proposition 5.6(a).

(γ = 0 if ∆ does not see f ). Enumerate rational G′0 and stop at the first G′ certified to satisfy (⋆):
Ex|f [− log G′ (Dn )] < Ex|f LP + 3 + γ for every f ∈ Y A . The left side is a computable real. The
right side is a finite sum of products of a computable nonnegative real P[path | f ] and an lsc value
LP (path) ∈ [0, ∞], hence lsc in [0, ∞] (with 0·∞ = 0). A true strict inequality “computable < lsc”
is certified in finite time, also when the right side is +∞; dovetail over candidates. By Step 2 some
rational G′ satisfies (⋆), so the search halts. If ∆ sees f , γ must be computed: Cdir is the maximum
over a simplex of finitely many terms u log(u/v), with u, v polynomial in ν and in computable values of ∆, and should be computable to within 1 via a modulus of continuity of entropy on a finite
alphabet; this is not written out, so this case is a proof sketch. Step 4. The procedure is uniform
in (P, ∆, n, A), so G′ has rational probabilities computable from these, and rejection sampling (undefined outside An ) makes it a function machine with K(G′ ) ≤ K(P ) + K(∆) + K(n) + K(A) + c.
(F1) gives Ex|f LSFI ≤ Ex|f [− log G′ (Dn )] + K(G′ ) + c ≤ Ex|f LP + 4 + Cdir + K(G′ ) + c; average
over f for the last claim. An almost surely halting reveal sampler with private memory has K(∆)
at most its complexity plus K(A) + c (Lemma D.3(c) uses the list An ).

Remark D.6 (What is open in Reading C). [open] history remarks after Cor 3.9; Q1, Q7; checks r6, r7, c3, r2 (i)
Whether K(n)+K(A) can be dropped: the minimax law depends on the horizon, and in one small
instance a direct computation certifies that one law for horizons 1–4 needs at least 0.026 bits (each
single horizon: at most 0.005); no growth was found up to horizon 6, weak evidence that K(n) is
an artefact. (ii) Defective samplers with K(P ) alone (ELP is then only upper semicomputable,
so (⋆) cannot be certified). (iii) Infinite alphabets at a fixed horizon. (iv) Reveal laws that see f ,
in case (b). The computed minimax value without the uniform component is at most 1.8 · 10−15
bits in all tested instances where ∆ does not see f .

Proof of Theorem 5.24 and Corollary 5.25. The left inequality is Lemma 5.2. For the right one
fix σ ′ and p attaining K pred (xσ′ ). The function machine T , which does not depend on p, on query
x enumerates the prefix-free set dom V (·, x) and takes the first element q found that is a prefix of
the tape; checking a candidate reads the tape only up to the first mismatch, which comes within
|q| bits, so T reads exactly q. It computes V (q, x) = ⟨x′1 , . . . , x′k ⟩ and runs TUH on the rest of the
tape at (x′1 , . . . , x′k , x). On tapes pω ′ , every x ∈ dom D finds p (as p ∈ dom V (·, x), a prefix-free
set), so all queries share ω ′ , and νT (D) ≥ 2−|p| MH (yσ′ ∥xσ′ ); apply (F1). For Corollary 5.25,
K pred (e-order) ≤ K(e) + c: given x, enumerate e until x appears.

Remark D.17 (What SFI can and cannot exploit). [remark (informal part not a theorem)] history §5.1
(not a theorem): SFI’s hypotheses answer each
query without seeing the other inputs, coupled only through the shared tape, so a label can
correlate with a guess about the other inputs (as the brooms and the optimal law for Theorem 5.26
do) but not depend on which inputs are present. Proved: gap(σ) := LSFI (D) − LH (Dσ ) is at
least maxσ′ LH (Dσ′ ) − LH (Dσ ) − c and at most min{K pred (xσ ), Lxin (xσ ∥yσ ), I(xσ : D)} + c, with
K pred (xσ ) ≤ K(x1:n−1 ) + c (Theorems 5.20 and 5.24, Corollary D.5, and Proposition D.16); in
expectation it is at most I(f → x) + K(ν) + c (Theorem 5.27). The bound I(xσ : D) is weak: for
constant labels it is about K(dom D), while LSFI = O(1).
remark, §5.2 “Gap bounds”, §5.5; check r3 Informally

Notation is that of Section 6. The coding theorem is used in the form of Section 2.1: if q(a | w) is
P
lsc uniformly in (a, w) and a q(a | w) ≤ 1, then K(a | w) ≤ − log q(a | w) + K(q) + c. “Gibbs”
P
is E[− log q(Z)] ≥ H(Z) for every q with z q(z) ≤ 1.

E.2

The cyclic permutation

For a string x and strings z = (z1 , . . . , zi ) put
D(x, z) := {(x, z1 ), (z1 , z2 ), . . . , (zi−1 , zi ), (zi , 0|x| )},

D(x, ()) := {(x, 0|x| )},

so that Dk = D(xk , (xk−1 , . . . , x1 )).
Lemma E.2 (chain semimeasure). [proved] examples Lemma 2.2 (history Lemma 2.0 with terminal label 0|x| ) For
P
every law ν of a random partial function F and every x, z ν(D(x, z)) ≤ 1, over the z for which
D(x, z) is a data set. Hence K(z | x) ≤ LSFI (D(x, z)) + c.
Proof. If F ⊇ D(x, z), then z is the orbit of x up to its first visit to 0|x| ; so for each F at most
one z qualifies. MF (D(x, z)) is lsc; apply the coding theorem.
Lemma E.3 (depth count). [proved; computed (examples e6(b))]
examples Lemma 2.2a For a partial function F and 1 ≤ k ≤ N − 1, let Ak (F ) be the set of nonzero
x ∈ {0, 1}d such that F (x), . . . , F k−1 (x) are nonzero d-bit strings and F k (x) = 0d . Then |Ak (F )| ≤
N − k, with equality for the broom Bk .
Proof. If x ∈ Ak (F ), the strings F (x), . . . , F k−1 (x) first reach 0d at times k − 1, . . . , 1, so they are
distinct, nonzero and not in Ak (F ): |Ak (F )| ≤ (N − 1) − (k − 1). For the broom, every nonzero
input off the spine is in Ak .
No violation occurs over all 625 partial functions for d = 2, all 88 total functions for d = 3,
and 105 random partial functions for d = 3, 4.
Proof of Theorem 6.3. (a) The lower bound is Theorem 2.21(a) with T = {0, 1}d , computable
from d. For the upper bound, the function machine T , on input x, runs V (·, {0, 1}|x| ) on program
bits read from the start of the tape and, if V halts with a table E of {0, 1}|x| , outputs E(x).
For fixed x the halting prefixes are prefix-free, so T is consistent; all inputs of length d read the
same program, so νT (f ) ≥ 2−K(f |d) ; apply Theorem 2.9. (The same argument gives LSFI (D) ≤
K(D | dom D) + K(e) + c whenever a partial computable e maps each x ∈ dom D to dom D.) (b)
Tcyc reads, for each length ℓ, its own sub-stream (ω⟨ℓ,i⟩ )i of the tape, draws a uniform ordering
of the nonzero ℓ-bit strings by rejection sampling, closes it into a cyclic permutation fˆℓ (the
bijection after Definition 6.2), and outputs fˆ|x| (x). It halts with probability 1, each value is fixed
by finitely many tape bits, K(νTcyc ) = O(1), and νTcyc (f ) = 1/(N − 1)!; apply Theorem 2.9. (c)
By (a), LSFI (f ) ≥ K(f | d) − c; apply Lemma 6.1 with Z = f , w = d. (d) Robbins’ bounds

Proof of Theorem 6.4. (a) If k−1 > 2ℓ −1, Bk answers 0ℓ on length ℓ; for k = 1 it maps everything

Conjecture E.5 (raw blocks). [conjecture] examples Conj 2.6a For raw blocks, SI’s expected label loss
is of order K(d) for typical d.
Heuristic, not proved. SI does not know where the first block ends. The label 0d is predicted
well by models with block length d, and by models with block length d′ > d only at a cost
P
d′ − d. So the first label costs about − log(m(d)/ d′ ≥d m(d′ )) = K(d) − K tail (d) ± c bits, and
K tail (d) ≤ K pad (d) + c = O(log log d).

Remark E.7 (where the information is). [proved; the other-direction part conditional]
examples Rem 2.7, 2.8, 2.12 A history-aware predictor that sees the previous pairs as a set predicts
perfectly: the most recent input is the unique input of Dt−1 that is not a label. So the separation
is between scoring a data set as a whole and predicting with the past in view, not between sets and
sequences; the previous inputs as a set are not enough (Proposition 6.8). In the other direction,
take X = Y = {0, 1} and yi = xi : LSFI ≤ c on every input sequence, while, if unnormalised M
has unbounded loss on the copied bits of some ω1 ω1 ω2 ω2 · · · (a form of the failure reported by
Lattimore et al. (2011)), SI’s unnormalised label loss with the identity code is unbounded on the
inputs ω1 ω2 · · · (Proposition 5.22).

E.3

Eisenstat’s example

Hänni’s e-mail argument, as quoted in his request (Hänni, 2026c), reads:
“Claim. log loss in your solomonoff function induction coinflip question is the loss given by
our threshold function, up to o(n) terms
Pf sketch.
easy direction: We know log loss of solomonoff function induction L_S is at most the log loss
of an appropriate threshold function L_T (up to a log n term for specifying the threshold),
because this guy lives inside the solomonoff distribution.
other direction: Say for a contradiction that L_S < 0.999 * L_T. Then SFI log loss on the
initial mixed coinflip segment L’_S would be even lower than that. But then you can specify
that coinflip sequence with only L’_S bits (by the standard pointing trick for going from a
solomonoff measure lower bound to a complexity upper bound). This contradicts a random
1/3 coinflip sequence being with very high probability incompressible below h(1/3) per bit.
So L_S approx L_T.”

In Remark 6.17, U Bn − τn H( 13 ) = 23 (GNn0 − 12 Nn0 ), with G as in the proof of Proposition 6.14.
Lemma E.8 (the heads frontier is rarely behind). [proved; computed (examples e7(a))] examples Lemma
3.5 P[ηn < τn ] ≤ 20e−n/18 . The exact values are 6.48 · 10−2 , 1.55 · 10−2 , 1.11 · 10−3 , 7.31 · 10−6 ,
4.27 · 10−10 at n = 10, 20, 40, 80, 160.
Proof. If ηn < τn , all revealed indices lie below τn , so τn ≥ n and b<τn has exactly Nn0 tails
(Lemma 6.11). So Nn0 ≥ n/2, or some k ≥ n has fewer than k/2 tails in b<k . By Hoeffding’s
inequality (Hoeffding, 1963) these have probabilities at most e−n/18 and e−k/18 ; the sum is at
most e−n/18 (1 + 1/(1 − e−1/18 )) ≤ 19.6 e−n/18 . The exact values come from a dynamic program
over the frontier process of Proposition 6.18.
Proof of Theorem 6.13. [0, gn ) ⊆ dom Dn with labels b<gn , and [0, gn ) and gn are computable
from each other; monotonicity and Theorem 2.21(a) give LSFI (Dn ) ≥ K(b<gn | gn ) − c. For b
Martin-Löf random for the computable P1/3 , the Levin–Schnorr theorem (see Li and Vitányi,
2008) gives K(b<g ) ≥ − log P1/3 (b<g ) − cb , so K(b<gn | gn ) ≥ − log P1/3 (b<gn ) − K(gn ) − cb ; if
τn ≤ ηn , then gn ≥ τn and − log P1/3 (b<gn ) ≥ U Bn . The upper bound is Proposition 6.12. By
Lemma E.8 and Borel–Cantelli the heads are eventually ahead; by the strong law ηn /n → 2 and
τn /n → 12 , so gn ≤ max(τn , ηn ) ≤ 3n for large n. Martin-Löf random b have probability 1.

Lemma E.9 (the Bayes predictor). [proved; computed (examples e1(c))] examples Lemma 3.8 Let h be a
history with frontiers τ, η, F := max(τ, η) and smallest unrevealed index g, and x the next input.
The conditional probability of label 1 at x is 0 if x < η and 1 if x < τ (for x < F exactly one
holds); for x ≥ F it is 1 if g < F and τ ≤ η, 0 if g < F and η < τ , and 2k /(2k + 1), k := x − F ,
if g = F (touching).
Proof. The first case is Lemma 6.11. Let x ≥ F . If τ ≤ η and g < F , the first unrevealed index
in [τ, η) is the next tail, so a tail reveal would give an input below η; the case η < τ is symmetric.
If h is touching, it depends only on ζ<t and b<F , so a head at F + k has probability 32 ( 23 )k 13 and
a tail there 13 ( 13 )k 32 ; the ratio is 2k .
A brute-force comparison over all 14178 reachable pairs (history, input) for n ≤ 5 finds no
difference.

87

Proof of Proposition 6.18. After each step the process is touching, or in a state (A, u) (heads
ahead, u ≥ 1 unrevealed tails in [τ, η)), or (B, u) (heads behind, u ≥ 1 unrevealed heads in [η, τ ));
indices ≥ F are fresh. From touching, a head reveal skipping k tails (probability 29 ( 23 )k ) leads
to (A, k), a tail reveal skipping k heads (probability 92 ( 13 )k ) to (B, k); k = 0 means touching.
From (A, u): u − 1 with probability 31 , u + m with probability 29 ( 23 )m . From (B, u): u − 1 with
probability 32 , u + m with probability 29 ( 13 )m . The A-walk moves down by exactly one and has
P
drift +1; its probability β of ever going one level down satisfies β = 13 + 23 m 31 ( 23 )m β m+1 , i.e.
2β 2 − 3β + 1 = 0, and β = 1 would contradict its convergence to +∞; so β = 12 , and (A, k)
returns with probability 2−k . The B-walk has drift − 12 and returns almost surely. The return
P
P
probability from touching is 29 + 29 + k≥1 92 ( 13 )k + k≥1 29 ( 13 )k = 23 , and after a touching step
the process starts afresh, so Ntouch is geometric. By Lemma E.9, a touching step costs P ∗ an
expected ϑ and other steps cost 0, so ELP ∗ (n) = ϑENtouch (n); exact dynamic programming gives
ENtouch (n) = 2.611, 2.907, 2.993, 3.000 at n = 10, 20, 40, 80.
Proof of Corollary 6.20. In (A, u) the unrevealed indices in [τ, η) are tails, so g is the next tail
to be revealed and a head lands at an index ≥ η > g. Almost surely Ntouch < ∞; after the last
touching step the process stays in A-states, since B-states return to touching almost surely and
A-states are left only through touching.
Proof of Proposition 6.21. At a touching step (g = F , input F + k) the rule errs on a head with
k = 0 (probability 29 ) and on a tail with k ≥ 1 (probability 19 ). It never errs in A-states and
always in B-states, where g is the next head. A B-excursion from (B, k) moves down by exactly
one with i.i.d. increments of mean − 21 ; optional stopping for the martingale ut + t/2 shows that
its length S is integrable, and Wald’s identity gives ES = 2k. So a touching step is followed
P
by k≥1 92 ( 13 )k 2k = 31 B-steps on average, and a cycle (a touching step and its excursion) has
2
1
1
2
9 + 9 + 3 = 3 expected errors. Cycles are i.i.d., Ntouch is a stopping time for them, and Wald’s
identity gives 3 · 23 = 2. For the second claim, the probability p of no error from a touching history
P
satisfies p = 92 p + k≥1 29 ( 23 )k (2−k p + 1 − 2−k ) = 13 p + 13 (an error-free step is a tail at k = 0 or a
head at k ≥ 1), so p = 21 . Exact dynamic programming gives 1.575, 1.891, 1.992, 2.000 expected
errors in the first 10, 20, 40, 80 steps; in 1000 simulated runs of length 1000 the rule erred 1.98
times on average and never in 48.8% of runs.

Remark E.10 (fresh noise). [proved (citing)]
examples Rem 4.7 Let the pairs be i.i.d. from µ × P (y | x) with fresh noise at each draw. If inputs
never repeat, fresh and frozen noise coincide and Theorem 4.1(a) bounds the regret against P by
K(P ) + c on every path. If some input has positive probability and P (· | x) is not a point mass
there, ELSFI = ∞ in both conventions (Proposition 4.6); time stamps or the per-query mixture
repair this (Theorem 4.5(b), Proposition 4.7).

E.5

Computations

The numbers in Section 6 come from seeded or deterministic Python scripts in the accompanying
repository, whose saved outputs re-runs reproduce: e1_eisenstat_exact (exhaustive enumeration in exact rationals, n ≤ 5, for Proposition 6.16 and Lemma E.9), e2_eisenstat_sim (Table 4, the rule’s errors), e3_cyclic (Table 3), e4_positive (Section 6.4), e5_dsl_odd_lengths
(Section 7), e6_cyclic_sharp (broom and chain laws against closed forms, 1092 exact comparisons; depth count; Robbins’ window; normalised and SI mixtures) and e7_eisenstat_dp (exact
dynamic program over the frontier process, cross-checked by exact rationals, enumeration and
simulation). Separately written implementations agree on the broom laws, the depth count, Robbins’ window, the SI codings and the dynamic program. The mixtures are finite, not SFI or SI;
statements about K, M , MF and MH are proved by hand.

Notation and conventions are those of Section 7; c, c′ are machine constants. We use the conditional coding theorem as stated in Section 2: a discrete semimeasure µ(· | z) that is lsc uniformly
in z satisfies µ(a | z) ≤ 2−K(a|z)+cµ .
Proof of Proposition 7.4. (a) Let V ′ (0u) := V (u), and let V ′ (1u) be an index of the function that

dom D, so each half of dom V ′ contributes W (D)/2: W ′ (D) = W (D). Every program 1u outputs
b on S, so W ′ (D ∪ b|S ) ≥ W (D)/2 = W ′ (D)/2. For all D: W (D) ≤ 2W ′ (D) by the programs 0u,
and W (D) ≥ 2−K(Q,b)−c W ′ (D) because V simulates 1u by the wrapper wzu of Proposition 7.1
and 0u by u.

Separating properties. Q = {x∗ } is decidable from x∗ , so sep(x∗ ; A | f ) ≤ K(x∗ | f ) + c.
Conversely, given f (hence the list B) and a decider of a separating Q, x∗ is the unique element
of B ∩ Q, so K(x∗ | f ) ≤ sep(x∗ ; A | f ) + c. Lower bound. Proposition 7.1 with b ≡ y and

of a decidable separating Q. This form is never sharper than the main bound by more than a
constant.
The grammar computation of Example 7.16(c). The script e5_dsl_odd_lengths.py (deterministic; code in the accompanying repository) computes an exact Bayes mixture over an enumerated grammar. A plain program is an arithmetic expression of depth at most 2 over the leaves
|x|, #1 (x), #0 (x), 0, 1, 2 with +, −, ×; an if program is “if C then E else E ′ ” with one of 80
simple conditions C (parity of a leaf, comparisons and equalities of leaves, first and last bit).
Programs with the same values on all strings of length 1 to 8 are merged into 1345 classes. The
prior gives weight pplain to plain programs and 1 − pplain to if programs, with fixed grammar
weights inside each. Training: all 170 strings of length 1, 3, 5, 7, labelled by their length; test: all
340 strings of length 2, 4, 6, 8. Only one plain class (“|x|”) fits the training data. Table 5 gives the
posterior predictive. The “tilted” rows add one program “if |x| even then 0 else |x|” with prior
mass equal to (or ten times) that of all plain programs.

Online, with pplain = 0.5: after all strings of length 2 are revealed with their labels, the
probability of a wrong answer at lengths 4 to 8 drops to about 2.7 · 10−3 ; after length 4, to about
1.5 · 10−8 ; after length 6, to 0 within the class. A single revealed string (“00”, label 2) leaves about
6.7 · 10−3 , because programs such as “if |x| even then 2 else |x|” survive it. A finite grammar
is not a universal machine, so this only illustrates Proposition 7.4, which is the proof that the
answer depends on the machine.

Remark G.1 (size of the constant). [proved] context Rem 3.1a With wi = 2−i and ni = 22 , the constant
2i

log(1/wi ) + (2nA − 1) log |Y | of Theorem 8.2(a) is of order 22 , triple exponential in the index i,
and i can be exponential in the description length of A. Weights wA = 2−ℓ(A) from a prefix-free
code make log(1/wA ) the description length, but the activation term remains. Hänni’s sequence
predictor starts machine i at the round ℓ1 with log log ℓ1 ≥ i (Hänni, 2026b), so his regret,
max(ℓ1 , ℓ2 ) bits, is doubly exponential in i. Length activation adds one exponential, because up
to 2ni distinct inputs are shorter than ni ; it buys a per-round cost that depends only on the current
input, as an x-only learner’s should. The round-activated Proposition 8.4 returns to the doubly
exponential form, in i and in cA . A hybrid that activates machine i at round ki but clocks by input
length has, on distinct inputs, regret at most log(1/wi ) + (ki − 1) log |Y | + #{j : |xj | < n′A } log |Y |
with n′A := min{n : g(n) ≥ cA }, by the proof of (a); it removes the extra exponential in i but not
in cA , and its per-round cost grows slowly with the round count.
Proof of Proposition 8.7 (regret). ñk and t are nondecreasing, so for k ≥ k0 and j ≤ k one round
of A at time j costs at most cA t(ñj ) ≤ g(k)t(ñk ). Hence in every learner round k ≥ k0 the budget
2g(k)t(ñk ) completes at least two further rounds of A (counting a partly done one), unless the
replay reaches the present. If rk is the number of rounds of A completed after learner round k,
92

then rk ≥ min(k, rk−1 + 2) for k ≥ k0 , so rk ≥ min(k, 2(k − k0 + 1)) = k for k ≥ 2k0 − 2. A caught(k)
up replay stays caught up, since one round costs at most the budget. So qi = A(· | hk−1 , xk ) for
(k)
(k)
k ≥ 2k0 −2; for the earlier rounds qi ∈ {A(· | hk−1 , xk ), u} and − log qi (yk ) ≤ log |Y |−log A(yk |
hk−1 , xk ). Sum as in Proposition 8.4. If the budget depended only on |xk |, a stream of short inputs
after a long one would leave a budget too small to replay even that one round, and the catch-up
could stall forever.
Proof of Proposition 8.12(b). For step n + 1 with n ≥ 1 and every ε, P[mistake] = E[errµ (hn )] ≤
ε + |Hf |(1 − ε)n . With εn := ln(|Hf |(n + 1)2 )/n this is at most εn + (n + 1)−2 . Summing over
P
steps 1, . . . , n0 (step 1 costs at most 1), with ln(n + 1) ≤ ln n + 1/n and n<n0 lnnn ≤ 12 ln2 n0 + 1e ,
gives at most 5.7 + (ln |Hf |)(1 + ln n0 ) + ln2 n0 . After step n0 , each mistake is made by the current
hypothesis, which lies in Hf , is consistent with the first n0 samples, is wrong at a point of positive
mass (so has error at least pmin ), and is eliminated by the mistake. The expected number of such
hypotheses is at most |Hf |(1 − pmin )n0 ≤ 1.
Proof of Theorem 8.10. (a) s>S 2−s = 2−S . If no circuit of size at most S agrees with f on
B, then Wcirc (f |B ) < 2−S , strictly because at every size some constant circuit disagrees with f
on B ̸= ∅; so S < LB . Take S = ⌈LB ⌉. Also Wcirc (f |B ) ≥ w(Cmin ) = 2− sizeB (f ) /NsizeB (f ) , and
log Ns ≤ s(4 + 2 log(d + 1 + s)).
P
(b) First inequality: Pi ≤ − ln(1 − Pi ), and i − log(1 − Pi ) = LB by Proposition 2.16 with
Wcirc (∅) = 1. Second: fix i and S := ⌈LB ⌉; let R and W be the circuits consistent with Di−1 that
give xi the right and the wrong label. The exception gadget at xi maps W≤S (size at most S)
injectively into R, since earlier inputs differ from xi , and Γ is increasing; so w(W≤S ) ≤ 2Γ(S) w(R).
Also w(W>S ) ≤ 2−S ≤ 2−LB = Wcirc (f |B ) ≤ w(R), because R contains every circuit that agrees
P
with f on B. So Pi /(1 − Pi ) ≤ 2Γ(S) + 1 and − log(1 − Pi ) ≤ Γ(S) + 2. In LB = i − log(1 − Pi ),
terms with Pi ≤ 12 contribute at most 2Pi (the chord from (0, 0) to ( 12 , 1)), and at most 2E indices
have Pi > 21 . So LB ≤ 2E(Γ(S) + 3).
Explicit form. γ ≤ d + 1 and d + 1 + S + γ ≤ 2d + 4 + LB , so LB ≤ R(LB ) with R(L) := 2E[(d +
1)(5+2 log(2d+4+L))+3]. R is concave and increasing with R(0) > 0, so {L ≥ 0 : L ≤ R(L)} is an
interval [0, L∗ ], and it suffices to show K > R(K) for K := 16aΛ, where a := E(d+1), Λ := X +Y ,
X := log(2(d + 2)) ≥ log 6, Y := log(E + 1). Since 6E ≤ 3a, R(K) ≤ 13a + 4a log(2d + 4 + K),
and 2d + 4 + 16aΛ ≤ 2(d + 2)(1 + 8EΛ) ≤ 2(d + 2)(1 + 8E)(1 + X)(1 + Y ). So it suffices that
P

12X − 4 log(1 + X) − 13 + 16Y − 4 log(1 + 8E) − 4 log(1 + Y ) > 0.

The first bracket is increasing in X and equals 10.65 at X = log 6. For the second, log(1 + Y ) ≤

and the bracket is at least 12Y − 4 log 4.5 − 4(Y + 0.087) ≥ −9.03. If E ≥ 1, then (1 + 8E)/(1 +

L∗ /(E(d + 1) log(2(E + 1)(d + 2))) = 10.85, at E ≈ 0.63, d = 1.

some cS = 1 with |S| ≥ k; by uniqueness of this representation, h depends on every variable of
such a monomial, which gives (i). For (ii), let h depend only on the variables in R, |R| = k. For
a ∈ {0, 1}R with a ̸= 1R , the input equal to a on R and 0 elsewhere has weight less than k, so
h = 0 there; and h(1R , ·) = h(1d ) = 1. So h = ANDR .
Proof of Theorem 8.14. Stage k (1 ≤ k ≤ d − 1) begins when the data are h(1d ) = 1 and h(x) = 0
for |x| < k, that is, after 1d , 0d and all inputs of weight below k have been shown with the labels
of f .
Formulas. A formula with s leaves depends on at most s variables. By Lemma G.2(i) every
function consistent at the start of stage k has formula size at least k; one of size exactly k depends
on exactly k variables and is a k-conjunction by Lemma G.2(ii). Every ANDS with |S| = k is
consistent and has a formula of size k. So the consistent functions of least size are exactly the kd
k-conjunctions, and Occam’s hypothesis is one of them, since description length increases with
size.
Circuits, k ≥ 2. In a circuit with s gates, let t ≤ s be the number of gates from which the
output can be reached. Each of the t − 1 reachable non-output gates occupies at least one of the
2t input slots of reachable gates, so at most t + 1 slots hold variables, and the function depends
on at most s + 1 variables. By Lemma G.2(i) a consistent circuit has s ≥ k − 1. If s = k − 1, then
t = s and exactly k slots hold variables, which must be k distinct ones; so the function depends
on exactly k variables and is a k-conjunction. ANDS has a circuit of k − 1 gates. So again the
least-size consistent functions are the k-conjunctions. (For k = 1 this fails: the one-gate circuit
x1 ∧ x2 is consistent at the start of stage 1.)
Every input of weight k is a mistake. For |S| = |S ′ | = k, ANDS ′ (1S ) = 1 iff S ′ ⊆ S iff
′
S = S. If Occam’s hypothesis in stage k is ANDS , then 1S is unrevealed (else ANDS would be
inconsistent), and the ordering shows it: Occam predicts 1, the label is f (1S ) = 0, and exactly
ANDS among the k-conjunctions is eliminated. So all kd inputs of weight k are mistakes, after
which stage k + 1 begins.

At k = d the argument shows that f has least size d (formulas) or d − 1 (circuits), so ℓ(f ) = d +
log(Cat(d−1) 3d−1 (2d+2)d ) for formulas, with Cat the Catalan numbers counting tree shapes, and
ℓ(f ) = (d−1)+log Nd−1 for circuits. Both are Θ(d log d): log((2d+2)d ) dominates Cat(d−1) ≤ 4d ,
P
and log Nd−1 = j<d (4 + 2 log(d + 1 + j)). So d = Θ(ℓ/ log ℓ) and the number of mistakes is at
least 2d−1 = 2Ω(ℓ/ log ℓ) for d ≥ 4. The trivial upper bound is min(2d , |Hf | − 1) ≤ 2ℓ+1 .
Remark G.3 (a second adversary). [proved; computed (context c11)] context Rem 3.11b For circuits the
count 2d − d − 2 also follows without the stage lemma, for every tie rule. Reveal 1d ; then, while
Occam’s hypothesis h has size(h) < d − 1, let Vh be the set of variables h depends on and reveal
1Vh . Each step is a mistake: by the slot count, |Vh | ≤ size(h) + 1 < d; h depends only on Vh ,
so h(1Vh ) = h(1d ) = 1, while f (1Vh ) = 0; and the point is new, or h would be inconsistent. The
phase reveals a new point at each step, so it ends, and then size(h) ≥ d − 1. Fix 2 ≤ k ≤ d − 1
and |T | = k, so size(ANDT ) = k − 1. While ANDT is consistent, Occam’s hypothesis precedes it,
so size(h) ≤ k − 1 < d − 1: the phase continues and reveals points of weight |Vh | ≤ k. When the
phase ends ANDT has been killed by a revealed point x with ANDT (x) = 1 ̸= f (x), revealed while
ANDT was consistent; so the ones of x contain T , x ̸= 1d and |x| ≤ k, hence x = 1T , revealed as
a mistake. So every point of weight 2, . . . , d − 1 is a mistake. A direct computation under seven
tie rules (d = 3, 4, 5) confirms this. (For formulas the slot count gives |Vh | ≤ size(h), and every
point of weight 1, . . . , d − 1 is a mistake.)

94

A direct computation (code in the accompanying repository) checks Lemma G.2 for all functions with d ≤ 4, the least-size statement for formulas and circuits with d = 3, 4, 5, and simulates
Occam on the ladder: 7, 15, 31 mistakes (formulas) and 4, 11, 26 (circuits) for d = 3, 4, 5, with ties
broken by truth-table index. Under seven tie rules every input of every level the theorem covers
is a mistake. The ratio of log(mistakes) to ℓ/ log ℓ stays between 0.64 and 0.82 (formulas) and
between 0.35 and 0.59 (circuits) for d = 3, . . . , 40.

The computations behind Remark 9.9 (d = 8 and d = 10; code in the accompanying repository): the eigenvalues at odd |S| ≥ 3 are below 3 · 10−18 in absolute value for both bias-free
two-layer kernels and nonzero with a bias or a third layer; the variance of ⟨z, χS ⟩ is at most
4.4 · 10−18 for |S| ∈ {3, 5} against 1.5 · 10−4 and 1.4 · 10−3 at |S| = 4; the identity of (c) holds
to relative error 6.2 · 10−12 , the training sum is at least 89.7 in every run, and the mean unseen
product falls from −0.10 (n = 100) to between −0.81 and −0.92 (n = 900). The sign accuracy
of kernel ridge regression on unseen points (d = 10, ten targets per cell, ridge 10−3 times the
mean diagonal) is 0.46–0.51, 0.37–0.41, 0.18–0.20 and 0.05–0.07 for n = 100, 300, 640, 900, while
controls with a nonzero eigenvalue (|S| = 4, or a three-layer NNGP with |S| = 3) reach 0.99–1.00
at n = 640.
